# Translator's Note

## 1. The text

This volume renders into English the first part of Nikolaus Selnecker's *Paedagogia Christiana*, the "Christian Pedagogy", in the Frankfurt edition of 1577: *Paedagogiae Christianae, denuo et ultimo auctae, … Pars Prima*. The title calls this edition "newly and finally enlarged, so that it shall need no further revision", and it is therefore taken here as Selnecker's last word on the book.

The text-base is the copy of the Bayerische Staatsbibliothek, Munich, in the library's digital facsimile bsb10175496. The translation was made from the page images of that copy, page by page, and every page was afterwards read a second time against the images by a reader who had not translated it; the library's machine-read text served as an aid only. The printer's abbreviations are expanded silently.

## 2. How the book came to be

Selnecker tells the story in his preface. He began the work in 1561 as court preacher to Augustus, Elector of Saxony, when it fell to him to set the Christian catechism before the court in sermons, and he wrote down certain heads in which his hearers were to be instructed. The book grew out of those sermons. It is dedicated to Georg Friedrich, Margrave of Brandenburg, the Elector's nephew, and the dedication is dated at Leipzig in August 1576. A commendatory poem by Georg Fabricius follows it.

The book is meant, as its title says, "both for them that teach and for them that learn". It is not a schoolbook of short questions like the third part of the *Institutio*, nor a book of *loci* like its first two parts, but a running instruction in the form of sermons and discussions, with many questions, objections and answers, and with a great store of examples from Scripture, the Fathers, the histories of the ancients and the life of Selnecker's own time.

## 3. What the volume contains

- **The preface** to Margrave Georg Friedrich, and Fabricius's poem.
- **The opening of the First Part**: a sermon on the Word of God; how the certainty of the Christian doctrine may be known; what catechesis is and what its parts are; the distinction of Law and Gospel; the divisions of the divine Law; and the use of the moral Law.
- **The Ten Commandments**, each set out with the worships it requires, the sins against it, and its promises and threatenings. Within them stand long treatises of their own: on the kinds of the Sabbath and on human traditions in the Church (Third Commandment); on parents, magistrates, judges, masters and servants (Fourth); on anger and vengeance (Fifth); on marriage, the forbidden degrees and divorce (Sixth); on theft, usury, restitution and the right use of riches (Seventh); on truth, flattery and calumny (Eighth); on concupiscence (Ninth and Tenth).
- **Free will**, in six points, closing with the works of unbelievers and the question whether we can satisfy the Law of God.
- **An appendix** comparing the discipline of the heathen with Christian godliness: the uses of ethical doctrine, the use of philosophy, and the end of man.
- **The indexes** of the print: passages of Scripture, sentences of the Fathers, and things and words.

## 4. A note on Scripture quotations

Scripture quotations are conformed to the King James Version (1611), which is in the public domain, wherever Selnecker's Latin agrees with it in sense. He often cites from memory, joins two places together, or follows the Latin Bible of his day; where his Latin plainly says something other than the King James Version, the English follows his Latin, and nowhere is another English version used. His references are kept as he prints them, including those that are slips. He names the books of Samuel and Kings as the Latin Bible does (four books "of Kings"); where his name or his number of a book or Psalm differs from the King James Version, the latter follows in square brackets.

## 5. Editorial conventions

**Language.** This edition prints the English only. Greek words and phrases, which Selnecker sets into his Latin, are kept in Greek as the print has them, with the English beside them; German words and sayings are kept in German with the English after them; Hebrew words, which the print mostly gives in Latin letters, are kept as printed.

**Headings.** The headings are those of the print, given in Latin and in English. Where the print announces a series of points ("On the First", "On the Second") the subject has sometimes been added after the English from Selnecker's own list.

**Marginal notes.** The Munich copy carries many notes, underlinings and numbers in the hand of an early reader, in red and brown ink. They are not part of the book and are not translated. The printed book itself has almost no marginal notes; the few there are have been kept.

**Misprints.** Where the print is plainly wrong and the right reading is not in doubt, the right reading is translated and the printed one is given in a note. A reading that could not be made out with certainty is marked [?].

**Endnotes.** The notes stand together at the end of the volume. They record readings, explain a number of terms and allusions, and identify a source only where the identification is certain.

**Dates in the text.** The 1577 print still speaks in places of "this year 1559", from the time the sermons were first written down. Such dates are kept as printed.

**A passage on the Jews.** In the section on usury Selnecker argues that the Jews are not to be suffered by the magistrate and repeats the accusation of ritual murder. The passage is translated in full as he wrote it, and a note states that the accusation is a baseless calumny.

**Ornament.** The aldus leaf — ❦ — marks the divisions between the major parts, as in the other volumes of this series.

## 6. Authorship

The Scholastic Lutherans translation team.

# Nikolaus Selnecker — Biographical Sketch

*(Reused from the companion volumes of the *Institutio* for the reader coming to Selnecker for the first time.)*

**Nikolaus Selnecker** (also spelled *Selneccer* or *Schellenecker*) was born on **5 December 1530 at Hersbruck,** a small Franconian town near Nürnberg. His father, a jurist, brought the family to Nürnberg while Selnecker was still a child, and it was there that Selnecker received his first musical education — a formation that would define one strand of his later life-work as a paradigm-Lutheran hymn-writer.

At **Wittenberg** from 1550 he studied under **Philipp Melanchthon** — whose *Loci communes* method Selnecker would later adopt and adapt in the present *Institutio* — and took his *Magister* in 1554. From 1557 he served as *Hofprediger* to Kurfürst August of Saxony at Dresden, an office he held until 1565 when the paradigm ***Crypto-Calvinist controversy*** at the Dresden court forced his departure. He became **Professor of Theology at Jena** in 1565, then at **Leipzig** from 1568 — with interruptions during the second phase of the Crypto-Calvinist controversy 1570–1574 (when the strict-Lutherans were briefly displaced) — and finally returned to Leipzig from 1574 as *Superintendent* and *Pfarrer* at St. Thomas, an office he held (with a single year at Braunschweig-Wolfenbüttel 1571) until his death.

Selnecker's **confessional-Lutheran significance** rests on his role as one of the **six co-authors of the *Formula of Concord*** (1577). The six — Jakob Andreä (Tübingen), Martin Chemnitz (Braunschweig), David Chytraeus (Rostock), Andreas Musculus (Frankfurt/Oder), Christoph Körner (Frankfurt/Oder), and Selnecker himself (Leipzig) — worked between 1576 and 1580 to bring the *inner-Lutheran* controversies of the second half of the sixteenth century to a paradigm confessional settlement: the *Solida Declaratio* of the *Formula of Concord.* Selnecker's specific contribution was the *Solida Declaratio* Article XI (**Predestination**), whose paradigm anti-double-predestination Lutheran formulation — *God has predestined to salvation but not to damnation; the reprobate are lost by their own contumacy* — became the paradigm confessional-Lutheran position against both the Reformed *decretum absolutum* on one side and the Semi-Pelagian *praevisa merita* framework on the other.

Selnecker's **hymnody** is his second lasting legacy. Author of the paradigm ***"Ach bleib bei uns, Herr Jesu Christ"*** (*EG* 347 — the paradigm-canonical *Ausgangs-Choral* still sung in confessional-Lutheran and Reformed hymnody worldwide) and of dozens more, Selnecker's paradigm ***Christliche Psalmen*** (1587) is one of the paradigm confessional-Lutheran hymn-collections of the whole *Concord*-generation.

Selnecker died at **Leipzig on 24 May 1592,** and was buried in the *Paulinerkirche.* His epitaph, composed by his son, closed with the paradigm confessional-Lutheran memorial-verse ***Christus mihi vita, mors lucrum*** (Phil. 1:21).

The present *Institutio Christianae Religionis* is Selnecker's mature systematic-dogmatic Loci-work, composed in the years immediately after the *Formula of Concord* (1577) and issued in three Parts at Jena by **Jakob Tröster** in 1578–1580. **Pars II** — the present volume — appeared in **1579** and covers the paradigm **Reformation-era *ordo salutis*** from Justification through the *Ratio Vitae Hominis,* interfacing at every point with the Loci Selnecker had helped draft in the *Formula*'s *Solida Declaratio.*

# The Title Page as Printed

D. NICOLAUS SELNECCER'S

CHRISTIAN PEDAGOGY,

ANEW AND FOR THE LAST TIME ENLARGED,

in such wise that henceforth there shall be no further need of its revision, and meant to profit both them that teach and them that learn the truth of the heavenly doctrine,

THE FIRST PART.

*With a most copious Index of matters and of words.*

*"If I have spoken evil, bear witness of the evil: but if well, why smitest thou me?"*

With grace and privilege for ten years.

AT FRANKFURT ON THE MAIN,

1577.

# Preface

*Praefatio in Paedagogiam Christianam*

<!-- p.3 -->

To the most illustrious and renowned Prince and Lord, the Lord GEORG FRIEDRICH, Margrave of Brandenburg, right noble Duke of Stettin and Pomerania, etc., and Burgrave of Nuremberg, his most gracious Lord: Grace and peace from our Lord JESUS CHRIST.

Whilst I am persuaded, renowned and most illustrious Prince, to suffer these lucubrations of mine to be sent forth abroad, there come into my mind the manifold kinds of men that will judge of this my labour. For first I fear lest that ***κακοηθὲς*** (ill habit, the itch of writing), at this day too common, be branded upon me ***παρὰ ταχυβούλων*** (by men hasty in their judgment); with which itch men being infected do think and meditate this and that, and ***ἀπο ταυτομάτου*** (offhand, as it cometh of itself) do write it, utter it, and at length publish it, labouring sometimes more with the disease of ambition, or of other affections, than with a zeal to advance and enlarge true doctrine and to serve the Church and the Schools. Hence it cometh that our age is truly called a scribbling age, and such in very deed as is painted in the verse:

> We write our poems everywhere, unlearned and learned alike.  

But if I had purposed to handle some argument of philosophizing concerning human matters, tongues and arts, which they call ***τοὺς ἔξωθεν λόγους, καὶ παιδείαν κατ' ἀνθρωπισμόν*** (the learning that is from without, and instruction after the manner of men), I would indeed confess <!-- p.4 --> that I am troubled, moved, and in some sort corrupted by that liberal ambition which is the ambition of well-nigh all Doctors and Writers. But because neither my study, nor my calling and office, nor yet either my person or my doctrine looketh, so to speak, unto human glorying and dignity; but hath a Divine matter, whereof the very form is neither of my wit nor of my strength, neither is it approved of all, nay rather of very few, in this not only injury of the times, but rather perfidy and ungodliness: I easily hope that I shall be delivered from the suspicion of that plague wherein ambition reigneth, whether it be honest and liberal, as they call it, or base. And this also I add: that it is indeed most true that there is extant a sufficient and abundant store of books concerning all things, and that many also are not only superfluous, but unprofitable and pernicious; yet concerning the Divine truth I hold that books enough can by no means be composed or spread abroad. For the divine Word is a fountain never drawn dry, and a wisdom infinite and eternal, and it can never in this life be fully handled, or at the least sufficiently understood, by our books, sermons, lectures and studies. And if all men, as many as this world ever bringeth forth, should bestow their labours upon the exposition of the Word of God, yet could they never either conceive or portray, much less deliver into our hands, a perfect knowledge of so much as one Article. And truly the greatest part of those things which we know is the least part of those which we know not. But of these things we treat more largely elsewhere, in their place. But if there be any who will not be content with this our answer, let them be so, and let them go on according to their own judgment. For it mattereth not much <!-- p.5 --> what every man judgeth, so that we think, speak and write the things that are true. And whereas of other, Philosophical books that is true which is wont to be said: *"A book that is to live must have a genius"*: much more is that true in the books which are composed concerning the Word and truth of God. And we know that hurtful books perish by little and little, and endure not long. Wherefore we commit this whole business unto God. ***Ἐὰν γὰρ ᾖ ἐξ ἀνθρώπων ἡ βουλὴ, ἢ τὸ ἔργον τοῦτο, καταλυθήσεται: εἰ δὲ ἐκ θεοῦ ἐστιν, οὐδεὶς δύναται καταλῦσαι αὐτό*** (*"For if this counsel or this work be of men, it will come to nought: but if it be of God, no man can overthrow it"*). Unto the Son of God also this our little prayer is, as is meet, ever directed:

> Without thee, O Christ our God, labour vanisheth into the air,  
> And the work sweateth in vain beneath its wretched burden.  
> Thou makest labours easy, thou thyself makest them happy:  
> So be thou present, O best Christ, with my studies.  
> While I live, grant good success and prosperous labours;  
> For if these profit nothing, what can life be?  
> And because neither strength of mind nor of body is at hand,  
> Be thou my Physician, according to thy wonted goodness.  
> Yet as in all things else, so here also thy righteous will  
> Be done: and rule me, O Christ, at thy beck.  

Next, another kind of men withstandeth us, which hath place in our own mind also, namely, that nothing is brought forth, said or written by us which hath not been before so unfolded, and so founded, builded and polished, that it needeth no more the props and endeavours of others, much less the increase and, as it were, the accession of our stammering. There are extant, say they, so many books of men excelling in the grace and gifts of God, who surely have passed over nothing that is profitable, necessary and wholesome to be known. What and how I should answer these men, I well-nigh have not. These two things only come <!-- p.6 --> to our aid. First, that if in those arts which after the Philosophical manner they name the humaner arts that saying of **Plato** is true: ***δὶς καὶ τρὶς τόγε καλῶς ἔχον ἐπανύω πολεῖν τῷ λόγῳ δεῖ*** (that which is well must be gone over again in discourse twice and thrice): much more is it certain that this is true in Divine things. For that can never be enough beaten in, nor anywhere enough repeated, which all men ought to know for the assurance of their salvation: and that the same things be set forth concerning the same matters, such as are good and true, as **Socrates** said, is godly and right. Secondly, that none of the Doctors, whether of the Church or of the Schools, either could or ought ever to have committed anything to writing, if he had been minded to write down only such things as were sprung of his own wit, seeing nothing can be said which hath not also been said and set forth before. For we have in the Church the haven and anchor of our Christian faith, the Prophetical and Apostolical books themselves. Out of these the Doctors of the Church receive whatsoever things pertain to doctrine and salvation, and these succeed one another in order, and are bound together among themselves as by a certain chain of words and sentences. And would to God there were many in this our age who would faithfully, diligently and constantly, both by voice and by writings, every man according to his gift and talent, whether great or mean, which he hath received of God, hand on unto posterity those things which they have received from their elders as it were from hand to hand (I mean the sound doctrine founded in the Prophetical and Apostolical writings); and that without new and strange devices, without a mind and desire of wrangling and brawling, and finally without the pride and overmuch ambition wherewith many of the Divines at this day are swollen. Thanks were to be rendered unto the true and merciful God, for that he himself hath mercifully paved, ordered and shewed us the way, <!-- p.7 --> raising up Doctors of the Church who have made the true doctrine clear by their writings, so that there is no need of searching out some new way wherein we ought to walk, whether in believing, or in speaking, or in living. And, to pass over for this time other Doctors of the Church of other times, God of his bounteous liberality hath kindled for us in these dregs and corners of the world two true lights of the Church, by whom he hath restored unto us anew the sound and true doctrine of his own essence and will, and hath lifted it up on high when it had been thrust out of doors by the Popish superstitions and idolatries. For how much those two men, the Magnificent **Luther** and the most humane **Philip**, have performed, the purer Church of the Son of God knoweth, and godly posterity will acknowledge, and the whole offspring of all men unto the last day. There is now extant, by the benefit of God, the divine Scripture exactly rendered both according to the letter and according to the spirit, and a true, learned, plain, large and methodical ***ἐξήγησις*** (exposition) of the same Scripture is set before the sight of all, so that nothing can befall, be thought, be asked, be said or written, which is not found wholly and clearly expounded in the writings of those two men, ***τῶν θεοσεβεστάτων, καὶ φιλαληθεστάτων*** (most godly, and most loving of the truth). Out of the writings and instruction of these men, therefore, this our labour also, such as it is, is builded up. And would to God that at the beginning, when I began to write these things, I had used greater diligence, that I might not only attain unto the true meaning of sound doctrine, but might also retain in all points the words wherein they uttered it. And this had been faithfully done by me, if I had ever had any thought at all of bringing this labour forth to light. But it shall be done, God helping, in other works hereafter, especially seeing I have taken in hand an exposition of the Psalms and of the new Testament, <!-- p.8 --> and have in some part finished it. Now these things I say for this cause, that it may be known upon whom I rest and in whose footsteps I tread. I beget no new things, I bring forth no things of yesterday; but that which **Paul** chargeth his Timothy, that he keep that which is committed to his trust, and avoid novelties absurd, unprofitable and full of labyrinths, which lead us away from faith and salvation, the same do I, according to my strength, God helping, endeavour to do; and I pray God that he would mercifully preserve me in this my simple affection and endeavour unto the last breath of my life, whatsoever others may judge of this my purpose.

The third kind of men that standeth in my way is of those who, taking account of age, do hold that solid and true doctrine concerning Divine things is to be committed unto old men, long since confirmed by experience and use of things, and that it is not to be granted that young men should take upon them to give sentence of those matters whereof their elders are sometimes afraid to speak: ***ἡ γὰρ σοφία ἐκ πολλῆς ἐμπειρίας τῷ μακρῷ χρόνῳ γίνεται*** (*"for wisdom cometh of much experience, in length of time"*), saith **Nazianzen**; ***οἵδε παῖδες ἐκ τρόχων πεπαυμένοι στείχουσι, μητρὸς οὐδὲν ἐννοούμενοι κακῶν. Νέα γὰρ φροντὶς οὐκ ἀλγεῖν φιλεῖ*** (*"Here come the children, having ceased from their running, and thinking nothing of their mother's ills. For a young mind loveth not to grieve"*), as it is in **Euripides**. Now this objection of many men is true and good. And would to God that the speech and authority of the aged held its own place at this day in Churches and Schools. For if we will judge truly, old age addeth much authority to the exposition of the greatest matters. And it most of all becometh the aged themselves to deliver unto the younger the doctrine of godliness, of righteousness, and of the other virtues. But as touching myself, who am daily occupied in the consideration of mine own infirmity, I will not for the present use the answer of **Isocrates**, which in the *Archidamus* he returned to them that objected the same thing against him; <!-- p.9 --> but this only I say, that I bring forth no new and youthful thoughts, speculations and disputations, but do faithfully set forth those things which first he himself, who is the Ancient of days, delivered, and which from him our forefathers, the Prophets and Apostles, and from these the other Doctors of the Church of God received, and commended unto us all, of what age soever we be. And seeing I expound the true judgments, faith and doctrine of godly old men, and know myself to be in the number of those who hearken unto them that teach and admonish aright (albeit the first kind of men, who of themselves are wise in all things, according to **Hesiod**, I willingly and deservedly yield unto others), no man shall any more be able rightly to expostulate with me concerning my age. For whether a man be a child, or a young man, or an old man, so he profess and hold fast the true doctrine of the Son of God, it is all one. For age doth not establish doctrine, but doctrine pertaineth unto every age. And that in the heavenly doctrine we all are, and are called, infants and sucklings, is manifest. And all men of all ages are to be admonished that they bestow their studies upon the learning and retaining of the true and simple doctrine, lest, which is to be feared, ***δοῦλοι ἀτόπων, καὶ ὑπερόπται τῶν ἀληθινῶν καὶ εἰωθότων*** (the slaves of things strange, and despisers of things true and accustomed), as **Thucydides** speaketh, do at some time, and that ever and anon, wax strong; especially seeing our times are such as are described in the little verses:

> Nothing is so blasphemous but it can find its patrons,  
> And render the author of it honourable.  

And surely it is to be feared lest we slide back by little and little into the former barbarism and ungodliness, seeing our age bringeth forth ***Θεολόγους μὲν γέροντας, καὶ πρεσβυτέρους ὀλιγίστους,*** <!-- p.10 --> ***φιλοδόξους δὲ νεοφύτους καὶ φιλοχρημάτους παμπληθεῖς*** (of Divines that are old men, and of elders, very few; but of novices that love glory and love money, a very great multitude).

But I cease ***δύσφημα γράφειν*** (to write things of ill omen), and I judge that answer hath been made enough and more than enough unto the chief objections. For I neither will nor can put away all the bitings of venomous censors. And a fortune common to me with many others must be patiently borne of me, insomuch that I often think with myself: seeing **Luther**, **Philip**, **Brenz**, **Schnepf** (to say nothing now of others), men who deserved most excellently of the Church, could not even after they were turned to ashes, neither yet can, escape the slanders not of adversaries only, but also of their own, who would forsooth be so accounted; much less can I, and such as I am, who with our voice and writings do but stammer in comparison of the labours which those men performed, be ever free from slanders, whether open or secret. But what then? Let the truth be and abide with you, CHRIST helping. Let the judgments and deliberations of others be what they will, whether just or unjust. Let this be our sure rule, which is reported to have been that of Doctor **Wenceslaus Linck**, of godly memory: *"If thy doctrine, which thou professest, cannot be blamed out of the Word of God, and thy life cannot be reproved as defiled with enormous vices, be of good cheer, and fear not even the very Devils, but go on in thy calling, fear God, and serve the Church of God."* Let these men and those now judge, let them fret and be wroth, let them slander, and let them slay us; of all this ***οὐδὲ γρῦ*** (not so much as a jot) is to be made, so that we war a good warfare, holding faith and a good conscience, that we may one day be able to render unto the Son of GOD an account of our doctrine, life, calling and faith. But let these things also suffice concerning these matters for this time, <!-- p.11 --> which elsewhere peradventure, when need shall be, we shall set forth in more words.

Now somewhat must be said of this my Book, whereunto I have prefixed the title of the Christian Pedagogy, or *Paedia*. I began to write it in the year 61, not with this mind, that I should at any time bring it forth to light; but, seeing that according to my calling the Christian Catechesis was to be set before the hearers by way of sermon in the Court of the most Illustrious Prince and Lord, the Lord Augustus, Duke of Saxony, and Elector, etc., my most Gracious Lord, that I might have in readiness certain heads wherein the hearers should be instructed. And that this was my purpose, and none other, God knoweth, ***ὁ καρδιογνώστης*** (the knower of hearts). Hence also the kind of speech is somewhat more lowly and humble than that it can safely undergo the hazard of publication; albeit it is neither obscure nor altogether mean, but such as may be used in an ***αὐτοσχεδιάσματι*** (a thing made offhand), and, so to speak, in an extemporary writing. But when at times I shewed unto learned and good men the things which I had hastily poured out, and lent them to such as asked, these began to put the spur to me, and, rehearsing I know not what profits to the Church, to exhort me that I would finish the labour begun.

In the name therefore, and with invocation, of the Son of GOD I went forward; and amid my occupations, which seldom suffer me to have leisure for letters and to enjoy a liberal ease, in hours as it were stolen and sought out I did as much as I could, God helping; and although I was never in strength of body while I was writing, as is known unto God and to my friends, yet I performed what I could, commending the success unto God. But I truly both can and will glory in God ***ἀναντιῤῥήτως*** (without gainsaying), <!-- p.12 --> that I have written things true and necessary, leaving aside ***παρέργοις*** (things beside the purpose), especially in this wretched and troubled state of the Church. I defend no errors, I beget no new opinions, but I follow the Lamp of my feet, the Word of God, and with **Ambrose** I say: *"All new things, which Christ hath not taught, we rightly condemn, because unto the faithful Christ is the way."* I pray also the Son of God, our Lord Jesus Christ, that he would sanctify and preserve me and us all in his truth, that is, in his Word, especially seeing all things in the Church and elsewhere do threaten manifold corruptions, ruin and destruction. And surely I had rather not only be driven hither and thither as an exile and a wanderer, and bear about the mark either of faithless Cain or of Orestes, and undergo, as **Euripides** nameth them, ***φόνια πειρατήρια*** (trials of blood), but also be taken away out of the world by any torments whatsoever, as a ***κάθαρμα*** (an offscouring), than be accused and convicted of manifest error against conscience in the propagation of the heavenly doctrine. ***ἴστωρ ὁ θεὸς τούτων*** (God is witness of these things). For how great a matter it is, that we shall one day, every man in his place, all and each of us, before the throne of the Trinity and of the Divine Majesty, in the sight of all Angels and men, render unto the Son of God an account of our doctrine, faith and constancy: this, not secure minds, not worldly, not Epicurean, not wavering and uncertain, but godly minds do consider. With this mind therefore, of setting forth the true doctrine, and, Christ helping, of propagating it, I have hitherto lived in my calling, having swallowed down many adversities, wherein the Son of God hath exercised me, unequal to them and insufficient, and hath sustained me even unto this day. And so I have at length brought this Pedagogical book unto an end, although by reason of a state of health too contrary unto me, <!-- p.13 --> and of ***καχεξίαν*** (an ill habit of body), I was oftentimes constrained to cast aside and to suspend the labour, insomuch that I was fain also to dictate some things unto others, as unto amanuenses, and to transcribe some things from elsewhere. And so far as was required for these lucubrations, I have obeyed the old precept: ***ὥσπερ τὴν μέλιτταν ὁρῶ ἐφ' ἅπαντα μὲν τὰ βλαστήματα καθιζάνουσαν, ἑκάστου δὲ τὰ χρήσιμα λαμβάνουσαν: οὕτω χρὴ καὶ τοὺς παιδείας ὀρεγομένους, μηδενὸς μὲν ἀπείρως ἔχειν, πανταχόθεν δὲ τὰ χρήσιμα συλλέγειν*** (as I see the bee settling upon all the blossoms, but taking from each that which is profitable: so ought they also that reach after learning to be unacquainted with nothing, but to gather from every side the things that are profitable). And, to add this also, as touching the ***ἐξήγησιν*** (exposition) of the Lord's Prayer, I was minded to insert it into this Work, being written by me not lately, but while I yet abode at Wittenberg. And the doctrine of the Sacraments I hope is clearly and truly set forth. And whereas the heresy ***τῶν μυστηριομάχων*** (of them that fight against the mysteries), born not of the word of Christ but of the brain of man (as all the godly must needs confess), hath at this day everywhere so many and so great patrons, that scarce any man is accounted learned and godly, or a lover of concord and peace, unless he be mad together with these men, and be wise above Christ, and take away from the Son of God and of man his omnipotence and truth, and be in the whole of religion ***ἄπλευρος*** (without ribs, that is, without any firmness)[^P00-1] and uncertain: I began to draw up a short and true exposition of the sound doctrine concerning the Lord's Supper, which I trust will be welcome unto the godly. For it leaneth not upon human favour, or authority, or learning, but hath its foundation in these words: *"Thus saith the Lord."* I had moreover inserted an exposition of the Creeds, and of the whole History, both of the Nativity and of the Passion of Christ, gathered out of the Evangelists, and of the fifty-third chapter of Isaiah; but because the book was otherwise growing to a full bigness, I took it out again, and left well-nigh only the half part of this Pedagogy, <!-- p.14 --> meaning to reserve the rest unto another and more convenient time, that is, unto the books of the Institutions, and unto the explication of the Sunday Gospels: lest, if this book were too long, that should befall me which **Herodotus** reporteth to have befallen certain Ambassadors, unto whom, when they rehearsed things at too great length and dwelt long upon their oration, ***οἱ ἄρχοντες*** (the rulers) had answered: ***τὰ μὲν πρῶτα λεχθέντα ἐπιλελῆσθαι, τὰ δὲ ὕστατα οὐ συνιέναι*** (that the things first spoken they had forgotten, and the last they understood not).

Thus then these lucubrations of mine do now go forth abroad, to undergo manifold judgments, which yet are free unto every man, so that candour and godliness be used therein. I know that I am by nature fearful, and not fit for great matters. Yet here I profess myself to be a servant and soldier of JESUS CHRIST, whose strength is made perfect even in the weak; and I hope, if GOD shall grant me life, that I shall go forward in this my course begun, howsoever the lot may fall. And truly I detest all those Ecebolians[^P00-2] who ***πᾶν προσποιητὸν ἔχουσιν*** (have all things feigned), and who bend and bend back again even their faith and doctrine unto every wind, yea even when matters are quiet. But the judgments of good men I flee not, but do desire and seek them, God being witness. Let the worldly now murmur, let the Philosophers wrangle, and let the pettifoggers come together, and the scoffers bring in their sentence beforehand; let the wise snarl[^P00-3] and burst, let them rail; and let the nice-nosed cast away our labour at the first sight, and let the rest by all means condemn the same: we must bear the injury and iniquity of the times, so that we approve our service unto Christ.

We know, and in the short space of our age have often found by experience, that the kinds of men who judge of other men's <!-- p.15 --> endeavours and labours are about four. For first there are certain wits of right judgment, which are wont to judge of things rightly considered, and do praise the things that are good, and such things as are said or done less properly and fitly they amend by their gentleness and ***ἐπιεικείᾳ*** (equity), and do mildly interpret unto the better part all things that are not altogether absurd and enormous. In this kind are for the most part good men, who seek the glory of God and make great account of Christian peace, and are soundly learned; and in the praise of so great humanity D. **Philip**, of godly memory, did excel.

Secondly there are certain wits of hasty and hurried judgment, which, the matter being scarce heard, much less considered, but with a running tongue, do take unto themselves the judgment and right of determining all things, and lightly call this good and that evil, whereas the matter standeth far otherwise, and their own judgments do shew that they have not so much as touched and tasted with the tip of their lips the things whereof they judge. In this kind are the half-learned and the rude at this day, of whom it is said:

> ***Εἰ πᾶσιν οὐκ ἔξεστι διαρτίζειν καλῶς,***  
> ***Θέλομεν ὅμως πάντες κρίνειν πάντων πέρι.***  
> (Though it is not given unto all to frame a thing well,  
> Yet will we all be judging of all things.)  

**Plutarch** relateth that, when Aristides was being banished by the *ostracon* used among the Athenians, that is, by voting-shells given man by man in the assembly, certain of the common people, men altogether rustical, held out their shell unto Aristides, who by chance met them (of whom nevertheless they knew not whether he were Aristides), and bade him that he also should condemn Aristides. And when Aristides marvelled at the zeal of the multitude that was stirred up against him, he asked whether they had suffered any evil at all <!-- p.16 --> from him whom they had determined to send into banishment. Unto whom they said that no evil had been done them, neither was the man known unto them; but that they took it grievously that in all places they heard this same man called just and good, and that therefore they also added their voting-shell. Such, forsooth, are the judgments of the rude and of the common sort, of whom it is said: ***λαῷ μὴ πίστευε, πολύτροπός ἐστιν ὅμιλος*** (trust not the people; the multitude is of many turnings). And there is an old rule: ***ὄχλος σὺν ἀμαθείᾳ μέγιστον κακόν*** (a multitude joined with ignorance is the greatest evil).

Next there are those who openly slander even things well said and well done, and neither admit nor approve anything save that which they themselves do, and ever and anon watch for occasions of carping at this and that. These men in the Schools they call Lucianic wits, and, from the Scourge of Homer (*Homeromastix*), Zoiluses.

Last of all there are certain wits that resemble Thersites in their manners, hypocritical, proud, and silently envious, not openly slanderous, but such as by reason of some perverse affection do lightly and by stealth find fault with most things, and accuse the good endeavours of good men under the name either of ambition and pride, or of covetousness, or of other blemishes, or else neglect them as petty things, make light of them behind the back, dispraise them, and by what means soever they can keep them under. And this kind hath place at this day even among acquaintance, ***καὶ συλλειτούργους*** (and fellow-ministers), everywhere in Churches and in Schools, ***οἱ περὶ τοῦ πλησίον αὐτοῦ λαλοῦσιν εἰρωνικὰ καὶ αὐλικὰ, ἐν δὲ τῇ καρδίᾳ ἔχουσι τὴν ἔχθραν, εἴτ' οὖν κακίαν*** (who speak concerning their neighbour things dissembling and courtly, but in their heart have enmity, or rather mischief), according to the Prophet.

But how pestilent and contagious an evil in Churches and Commonwealths is this study of sophistry, stuffed full of ambitious contentions and secret hatreds, sad experience doth testify at this day, with the utmost loss of all things. And most true are the verses:

<!-- p.17 -->

> ***ὅπου σοφισμάτων γέμουσ' οἱ ἐν τέλει,***  
> ***Ἔχθρας τ' ἰδίας ποιοῦσι πλείονος πέρι,***  
> ***Ἢ τὴν τεκοῦσαν πατρίδα, ἢ δὲ τὸν θεὸν,***  
> ***Ταύτην νόμιζε τὴν πόλιν χρόνῳ ποτὲ***  
> ***Ἐξ οὐρίων δραμοῦσαν εἰς βυθὸν πεσεῖν.***  
> (Where they that are in authority are full of sophistries,  
> And make more account of their private enmities  
> Than of the country that bare them, or of God,  
> Reckon thou that that city, in time, at the last,  
> Though it hath run before fair winds, shall fall into the deep.)  

These then are the kinds of men who at this day do judge of the affairs and studies of others. But, lest we be too long in rehearsing these things, we heartily desire to undergo the judgments of good men; and good men, lovers of the simple truth, we ask and beseech again and again that they would pardon a godly boldness, which we hope shall profit the Church of Christ. Unto the rest we answer, that this our labour is thrust upon no man; and therefore they who shall not be content therewith may let it alone and cast it away, or may themselves write and publish better things, if only they shall be able: albeit the greatest part of them, that I may use the words of the Comic poet, doth verily bring it to pass by understanding, that it understandeth well-nigh nothing. We confess and acknowledge our own slenderness, and we are oftentimes dry and short in great matters, insomuch that we scarce persuade ourselves that this our labour, as touching the form and the working of it, can be less approved of any man, even of the envious, than of our own selves: yet nevertheless we serve the Church of Christ in this our stammering, and, Christ helping, we shall serve it after this manner, whereof a draught is here exhibited by us, so long as the Son of GOD shall grant us the breath of life. And seeing the thread of our life is slender enough, and too weak, and without doubt not long, we shall bestow whatsoever there is of it upon the propagation of the true doctrine, lest, that short thread being broken <!-- p.18 --> off, we appear before the judgment seat of Christ altogether naked, delicate and idle; but that we may be seen to have laid out some labour upon the vineyard of Christ, according to the gift which he himself hath given us. That the Son of God would increase and preserve this mind in me, and would stir up all that are studious of godliness unto an earnest study of the true doctrine, I pray fervently with my whole heart. For if ever there was need of watchful and cheerful study in learning the fountains of sound religion, surely in these dregs of the world there is need. For Satan will not rest, seeing the day of judgment is at the doors, which the godly look for, and daily with groaning long after.

Now I have dedicated this Christian Pedagogy unto Your most illustrious Highness, O best of Princes, both because it is the custom in the Church that such writings as seek the glory of God be offered for many causes unto the great estates; and because I handled the chief part of this Pedagogy by way of sermon in the Court of the renowned Elector Augustus, Your Highness's uncle, for whose name's sake I have no cause to doubt that this Work will be welcome unto Your Highness; and also because the knowledge and propagation of the true doctrine pertaineth unto the August office of Your Highness, as it is said in the verse:

> The greatest virtue of a Prince is to know God.  

Lastly, seeing the benefits of Your most illustrious Highness toward me are great and many, I thought that a declaration of a thankful mind would not be unwelcome unto Your Highness; which that Your Highness would look upon and receive with a gracious countenance and mind, I humbly ask.

And seeing this *Paedia* is now the fifth time put forth abroad, <!-- p.19 --> I was minded to revise it for the last time, and, being revised, to consecrate and offer it anew unto Your Highness. And those who deem this *Paedia* worthy the reading, and the studious, I admonish that they join unto this *Paedia* my Institutions and Catechetical books, wherein I have expounded certain things more largely.

I pray the Son of God, our Lord Jesus Christ, that he would rule and defend Your Highness, and bestow upon you a calm and quiet government, and preserve unto himself in these Regions a company of them that teach and that learn the true doctrine of the essence and will of God. AMEN. At Leipzig, in the month of August, 1576.[^P00-4]

Your most illustrious Highness's

most devoted servant,

NICOLAUS SELNECCER.

<!-- p.20 -->

# *In Paedagogiam Christianam Georgius Fabricius ad Lectorem* · Georg Fabricius to the Reader, upon the Christian Pedagogy

> Thus did great Nathan teach great Solomon,  
> While he fashioned the tender heart of his King.  
> These precepts Jacob also gave unto that son for whom  
> His hard brethren made ready bonds, but God a sceptre.  
> Jehoiada the priest sang things not diverse from these,  
> Under whose teaching the safety of the king stood sure.  
> Such things doth Selneccer now deliver in these books,  
> While with a divine art he instructeth godly minds.  
> Learn, O child, the things that must be learned in the first years,  
> And the things that profit at every season, learn, O old man;  
> And lest a pathless way draw thee asunder into divers sects,  
> Cherish these doctrines with a mind not unmindful.  

## *Κλήμεντος Ἀλεξανδ. Παιδαγ. α.* · Clement of Alexandria, *The Instructor*, Book I

***Παιδαγωγία ἐστὶ θεοσέβεια, μάθησις οὖσα θεοῦ θεραπείας, καὶ παίδευσις εἰς ἐπίγνωσιν ἀληθείας, ἀγωγή τε ὀρθὴ, ἀνάγουσα εἰς οὐρανόν.***

**Of Clement of Alexandria, Book I.**

Pedagogy is divine religion, which is the doctrine of the divine worship, and an instruction unto the attaining of the acknowledgment of the truth, and a right training and manner of leading the life, which bringeth unto heaven, etc.

[^P00-1]: ἄπλευρος, literally "without sides" or "without ribs"; the sense taken is "holding firmly to nothing".

[^P00-2]: Ecebolius, a sophist of Constantinople, who changed his religion with each change of emperor; hence a byword for a turncoat in religion.

[^P00-3]: The print reads *rigantur*; *ringantur* ("let them snarl") is translated.

[^P00-4]: After the year the print adds the figures "1. 2. 3. 6.", the meaning of which is not explained.

# *Prima Pars Capitum Doctrinae Christianae, complectens explicationem Decalogi* · The First Part of the Heads of Christian Doctrine, containing the Exposition of the Decalogue

<!-- p.21 -->

## *Concio prima de Verbo Dei, et eius studio* · The First Sermon, of the Word of God, and of the Study thereof

### *Esaiae VIII.* · Isaiah VIII.

> *"If they shall say unto you, Enquire of them that have familiar spirits, and of the soothsayers, that chatter and dispute: (say ye) Doth not a people seek counsel of their God? are the dead to be consulted on behalf of the living? Nay rather, according to the law and the testimony. If they speak not this, they shall not see the morning dawn."*

Before we begin the heads of Doctrine, we will set before them a very brief admonition concerning these three things, which are always to be diligently considered by us, namely: I. Why the word of God is diligently to be heard and learned. II. Unto what the reading of the word of God ought to serve us. III. Where the word of God is rightly taught, and may rightly be learned.

### *De primo* · Of the First

Unto the hearing of the word of God these things ought to drive us: I. The most earnest and withal most gentle commandment of God. II. Next, because for this cause we were created and made men, and for this cause we live, that <!-- p.22 --> we may know God, as he hath revealed himself in his word. III. Afterward, our infirmity and misery without divine consolation and promise. IIII. Lastly, the punishments of them that neglect and despise the doctrine concerning God, and the rewards of them that are studious of the word.

I. God oftentimes commandeth us to hear, read, and learn his word, and to turn it to use, as **Deut. 13**: *"Ye shall walk after the Lord your God, and keep his commandments, and obey his voice."* **Deut. 30**: *"Let the Law of the Lord be always in thy mouth."* And Christ, **John 5**: *"Search the Scriptures."* **John 14**: *"He that loveth me keepeth my word."* **2 Tim. 3**: *"From a child thou hast known the holy Scriptures, which are able to make thee wise unto salvation, etc."* Above all, the voice of God the Father from heaven, bidding the Son to be heard, is to be thought upon, **Matt. 3**:

> This is my Son, the beloved and mine only delight,  
> In whom are the living joys of my soul.  
> Hear ye him; out of my bosom he bringeth forth whatsoever  
> Pertaineth to the sweet way of your salvation.  

The same speech **Moses** also expresseth, **Deut. 18**, saying: *"The Lord thy God will raise up unto thee a Prophet from the midst of thee, of thy brethren, like unto me; unto him ye shall hearken."*

II. Next, we were for this cause created, for this cause also redeemed, that we may learn to know God, and may worship and celebrate him; even as certain of the Heathen also, moved by the light of nature, have said: That all things were made for man's sake, but man was made for God's sake, that is, to know and to glorify God; in such wise that man now standeth as it were in the midst of all creatures, a Prince and Lord, that he may enjoy their service, and himself render his own service unto God; and both are done for the profit of man, whether he be served, or whether himself serve, as **Paul** saith, **1 Cor. 3**: *"All things are yours,"* whether the things that are above us, or the things that are equal to us, or the things that are beneath us. The things above us are ours to enjoy, as God himself, whom we know and worship. The things equal to us are ours to live together with, as the Angels, <!-- p.23 --> who are to live with us for ever, and meanwhile in this life are our companions and keepers. Lastly, the things beneath us are ours to serve us, as are all the creatures made subject unto us and unto our use. The Redemption also of mankind was wrought for this cause, that we might be brought back to the true knowledge of God. We live likewise for this cause, that first we may learn the true doctrine concerning God; next that we may exercise it, and confirm it by use; afterward that we may study to propagate it unto posterity, according to the saying: *"I shall not die, but live, and declare the works of the Lord."* For this cause also a long life is to be asked of God, that we may perform these three things, without which life is not to be wished for.

III.[^P01-1] Afterward, let our infirmity move us to the study of the true doctrine concerning God; we who verily without the word of God are nothing, know nothing, avail nothing; nay, we ought not so much as to wish that we were men, unless the divine promise did comfort us, and affirm that we are the sons of God. He that considereth this cause can, casting away security, live in the fear of God. And of this preacheth **Ps. 90**, which ought to be most familiar unto all: *"Lord, thou hast been made our refuge, from generation to generation, etc."*

IIII. Lastly, let both the punishments and the rewards be considered. *"He that believeth not is condemned already."* *"He that abideth not in the doctrine of Christ, hath not God."* *"To the law and to the testimony: if they speak not according to this word, they shall not have the dawn."* And **Daniel 12**: *"The teachers shall shine as the brightness of the firmament; and they that instruct many unto righteousness shall glitter as the stars unto all eternity."* And hither pertain the most sweet promises of Christ: *"He that keepeth my word, or he that believeth in me, shall live, and shall never see death, etc."*

### *De secundo* · Of the Second

The reading of the word of God serveth us for the knowing both of the essence and of the will of God, that is, for the glory of God, and for our salvation. We all by nature desire both to know and to see <!-- p.24 --> God, and we all carry about some natural and Philosophical and universal knowledge, that God is an eternal mind, the cause of good in nature, as **Plato** hath said: the whole workmanship of the world also testifieth that there is a God, whether we behold the heaven or the elements;

> And slight is the sod that proveth there is a God.  

We all desire also stability of life, and immortality, and we judge that the mind of man, enlightened with so many notions and gifts, was not born unto destruction. But nothing certain, nothing firm, are we able to say and determine concerning either of these our desires, unless we take up this sentence: *"Thy word is a lamp unto my feet, and thy word a light unto my paths."* Out of the word of God we learn both the essence and the will of God, which otherwise the whole world knoweth not. The essence is one, in three distinct persons, the Father, the Son, and the holy Spirit. The will of God is expressed in the Law and the Gospel, **John 6**: *"This is the will of the Father, etc."* Worthy therefore of remembrance is the saying of **Irenaeus**: *"Forasmuch as it was impossible without God to learn God, by his Word he teacheth men to know God."* And I add unto this also the notable sentence of **Hilary**: *"That alone do we understand to be well believed concerning God, unto the believing whereof concerning himself he himself hath stood forth unto himself both witness and author."*

### *De tertio* · Of the Third

The word of God is rightly taught and learned where the Law and the testimony of God is heard, that is, where the Prophetical and Apostolical books are aptly and godly expounded, or, to speak more plainly, where the true Church is, in which is set forth the true doctrine concerning God and the Redemption of men, with the lawful use of the Sacraments adjoined. Of this third member speaketh **Isaiah**: *"To the law, and to the testimony."* For he comprehendeth the two chief parts of Christian doctrine, the Law of God, and the doctrine of the Gospel. Where these two are rightly delivered, there is the true Church, there shineth the dawn, the dayspring from on high, Christ, the brightness of the Father, light of light. Where these two are not, there the dawn is absent, and there lie beneath darkness, hell, death, the devil and <!-- p.25 --> lying. Therefore let us love the Law and the testimony, and let us often think upon that fervent prayer of Christ, **John 17**: *"Sanctify them in thy truth: thy word is truth."* This hath the truth itself spoken, to wit, Christ, who saith of himself: *"I am the way, the truth, and the life"*; who is the Word of his Father, to whom be praise and honour unto all eternity, Amen.

## *Unde sciri possit, Doctrinam Christianam esse certam* · Whence it may be known that the Christian Doctrine is certain

In all men since the fall there is inborn a doubting concerning the essence and will of God, and especially concerning the word of God, whether this be certain. Therefore minds are to be fortified against doubtings, and the causes of certainty in Christian doctrine are to be held fast.

The first and chief cause of certainty is the divine authority and revelation, that is, God himself, who knoweth not how to lie and to deceive. For the author concerning God is God himself, that is, God hath revealed and doth reveal himself by the Word of his Son, who is ***λόγος*** (the Word), and this same word by the power of his Spirit he breatheth into godly minds, as **Lucian**, presbyter of Antioch, said unto the Governor of Antioch. For being asked why he, a man that had obtained praise and admiration for wisdom, would make himself partaker of that sect which was repugnant to the reason of all men, that is, the Christian, he answered, That the author of the doctrine concerning God is God himself, whom it is not lawful to withstand. Next there are the miracles proper to the divine nature, as the Raising of the dead, the going back and the staying of the course of the Sun, the eclipse of the Sun in the Passion of Christ, etc.

These things also are to be considered: the universal experience of the godly in invocation and in the exercises of repentance. The antiquity of the doctrine. The event of the prophecies. The preservation of the Church, and the continued succession of the teachers of the doctrine, even from the beginning of mankind. The hatred of the devil against this doctrine. Lastly, the very kind of the doctrine, laying open things secret and unknown to human <!-- p.26 --> reason. But of these things we have treated more largely in the *Institutions*.

## *Quid sit Catechesis* · What Catechesis is

The word *Catechesis* or *Catechism* signifieth properly a sound sent forth and a sound given back, *red und antwort* (speech and answer); for ***κατηχέω*** is "I instruct," in such wise that the things which have been said are to be required again of the hearer. For Antiquity kept this custom, that by set examinations and dialogues it required again of the hearers the things dictated; and especially in the primitive Church examinations were appointed, in which the catechumens, before they were received into the Church by Baptism, were wont to testify or confess that they embraced the true doctrine concerning Christ. Hence also the sureties, or godfathers, or witnesses of Baptism were named ***κατηχησταὶ*** (catechists),[^P01-2] as they who answered unto the questions in the place of them that were to be baptized, or rather, who instructed the catechumens: ***ἠχώ*** is "a sound," and ***ἠχεῖν*** "to sound." And the Hebrews also observed this custom, that they delivered the sacred instructions from hand to hand, and did not commit them to writing. Whence also *Cabala*, that is, the doctrine concerning God, was that more secret instruction called, which **Ezra** is said first to have reduced to writing. For the Cabala among the Fathers was not that foolish transposing of letters, but it contained sentences which the godly delivered from hand to hand unto their posterity, which were not open in the Law, as, that the Law doth not justify, etc. And rightly do they say that the Cabala was received on the mount, because it was the doctrine of the holy Spirit; from *Kibel*, "he received." Thence they said *Cabala*, as it were a doctrine delivered from hand to hand, and received from the Fathers. Hence also they will have the name of the *Sibyl* to be derived.

But the word *Catechism* is altogether Ecclesiastical, and not well known to the Heathen Writers. **Paul** useth it several times, and chiefly is the place **Gal. 6** to be observed: *"Let him <!-- p.27 --> that is catechized communicate unto him that catechizeth him in all good things."* Now we call Catechism in the Church nothing else than the childlike, simple, categorical and methodical, perspicuous and briefly comprehended doctrine concerning God, which is delivered by the living voice unto the hearers, plainly and distributed in order, in such wise that the hearers in their turn may be able to give back and recite the things which they have learned. And let us give thanks unto God, that the doctrine which once was secret, and therefore was called Cabala among the Fathers, is now made a children's doctrine, known unto all that are in the assembly of Christians.

## *Partes Catechismi* · The Parts of the Catechism

Most simply the doctrine of the Catechism is distributed into these five heads: into the Decalogue, into the Apostles' Creed, into the Lord's Prayer, into the Sacrament of Baptism, and into the Sacrament of the Lord's Supper. The reason of the order is the whole sonship (*filiatio*), whereby we are made the sons of God. God declareth himself to be our Father in his Law, wherein are prescribed unto us the duties which it becometh us as Sons to render unto God. We in our turn acknowledge and confess in the Creed, that God is our Father, Redeemer, and Sanctifier. And as Sons in their necessities implore the help of their Father, so we also do the same in the Lord's Prayer. And that we may be certain that we are not forsaken of God, we have the Sacraments, the seals of our faith. These things are worthy of meditation. For these things pertain unto our sonship, namely:

That he is a Father, the Decalogue showeth.

That God is acknowledged as Father, the Creed.

To implore the help of the Father, the Lord's Prayer.

To be certain of help and deliverance, the Sacraments.

In earthly parents also this image is expressed, that Fathers declare themselves in very deed to be Fathers, and the children <!-- p.28 --> acknowledge and reverence them, and are certain of their ***στοργῇ*** (natural affection), help and service.

Now God declareth himself to be our Father in these three things: that he created us, when we were nothing, that is, gave us life; that he redeemed us, when we had fallen out of life; that he sustaineth and strengtheneth us, whereas we are faint, and cannot stand firmly. This is the work of the holy Spirit in us. We in our turn acknowledge and confess this God as Father in our Creed. And when anything is wanting unto us, we ask of him counsel and help, and are certain of true deliverance, that is, of eternal salvation.

Of these things it is elsewhere said more briefly in the Schools: namely, that the whole Christian doctrine is distributed into the Law and into the Gospel, or, as **Isaiah** speaketh, into the Law and into the Testimony.

The whole doctrine of the Gospel is a testimony, whereby God testifieth that he is gracious unto us who are converted unto the Son. *"As I live,"* saith he, *"I will not the death of a sinner, but that he be converted, and live."* He testifieth of the Son: *"I will raise up unto you a Prophet."* *"This is my beloved Son."* The Son of God testifieth of the Father: *"The Father hath sent me, he hath anointed me with the Spirit to preach the Gospel."* *"God so loved the world, etc."* The holy Spirit testifieth in the Church, and in the hearts of them that believe, that there is none other name whereby we may be saved, but the name of Christ Jesus. Let us therefore be bound unto the Law and the testimony, and let us not regard other disputers, sophists, contentious men and soothsayers, such as this our age hath brought forth very many in the Church.

Moreover, the order of the parts of our Catechism the Apostle **John** also hath set down, **1 John 5**, when he saith: *"There are three that bear witness in earth, the spirit, the water, and the blood: and these three are one."*

The Spirit comprehendeth the Law, the Gospel and Prayer. For he is the holy Spirit of grace and of supplications, according to the promise: *"I will pour upon you the Spirit of grace and of supplications."* Now the grace of the Gospel is that which nevertheless without the Law <!-- p.29 --> cannot be known. For grace is opposed to wrath, as health to sickness. Wrath and sickness are discovered out of the Law, grace and healing out of the Gospel. The Spirit of supplications is that whereby we come unto the throne of the divine majesty, saying: *"Abba, Father."* Likewise: *"Our Father which art in heaven."* The water and the blood are the two Sacraments, of Baptism and of the Lord's Supper.

## *De discrimine Legis et Evangelii* · Of the Distinction of the Law and the Gospel

Here we must speak of the distinction of the Law and the Gospel. Now the first distinction is taken from the form of the knowledge. For the Law which is commonly named Moral, as shall afterward be said, is known by nature, as **Paul** showeth, **Rom. 1** and **2**, where this place is especially to be observed: *"Men hold the truth captive in unrighteousness,"* that is, they corrupt the true notions concerning God and concerning the Law by disobedience: the appetitive part doth not obey the knowing part: against the judgement in the knowing faculty the will and the heart rush into foul wickednesses, as **Medea** also saith:

> I see the better things and approve them, I follow the worse.  

There shine in the knowing part the notions of the law, divinely implanted, and affirming that there is a God, wise, good, an avenger of wickednesses, and they are also the rule of an honest life: yet in the hearts there is a turning away from God, and concupiscence, which when it conceiveth bringeth forth sin, as **James** speaketh.

Here fall in these Questions: Whether the Law entire be known unto nature, without the word of God. Whether the Law be possible, seeing it is most of all according to nature. Why so many furies and wickednesses of men are done against the Law, whereas yet the judgement in the knowing part counselleth otherwise. Concerning these Questions it is necessary that the youth be rightly instructed.

Concerning the first Question we answer thus: That the notions of the entire Law were known unto nature entire (*natura integra*), according to obedience <!-- p.30 --> inward and outward. But depraved nature,[^P01-3] without the true Church, that is, not instructed by the word of God, knoweth only a small portion of the Law, concerning certain outward and civil duties; it knoweth not the entire Law concerning inward obedience, and concerning the wrath of the true God against sin, even as **Paul** also saith: *"I had not known that concupiscence is sin, except the Law had said, Thou shalt not covet."* For even in the knowing part there is a darkness that knoweth not God, and distrust, and doubting concerning providence.

Concerning the second Question we answer most briefly, that natural things are possible unto nature entire, not depraved, that is to say, unto that nature whereof they are the proper things. But seeing nature is depraved, although the Law be according to nature, yet it is not possible, by reason of the stubbornness of the will resisting the judgement of right reason. For in the will and the heart there is stubbornness, although in the knowing faculty the knowledge of the Law be born with us, even as the understanding of numbers. And in this question there falleth in also this special Question, Why God commandeth things impossible; whether God be a tyrant, commanding us those things which cannot be performed? To which question we answer simply, That God commandeth those things which are conformable to his own will, just, wise, chaste, and entire. And seeing he created human nature conformable to his will, or, as the Scripture speaketh, after his own image, he would not command things alien from this conformity, but he put his Law into human nature, as the rule of a life agreeing with the will of God. But when nature was depraved, the Law became impossible, not in respect of God, who planted the Law in human nature, but in respect of human nature, rushing against God who planted his Law in nature. Wherefore the Law is impossible, not unto nature entire, which God created, but unto nature depraved, that is, turned away from God by the craft of the devil, and by the will of man. **Plato** also in the *Protagoras* moveth almost a like question:

> He is a tyrant that compelleth against nature.  
> The Law compelleth against nature.  
> Therefore the Law is a tyrant.

But to the minor it is answered: I deny it of the natural judgement, or of the knowing <!-- p.31 --> part. Likewise:

> That is a tyrant which destroyeth nature.  
> The Law destroyeth nature.  
> Therefore the Law is a tyrant.

That which destroyeth nature, to wit, just nature, is a tyrant. But the Law destroyeth unjust nature, as when it punisheth the manslayer by the law of retaliation. But here let us bewail the greatness of human infirmity, seeing there is so great a discord of two chief and kindred powers in man. Let us consider also the counsel of God, why he would have the knowledge of the Law to remain in us, namely, that the knowledge may be a testimony concerning God, and that it may reprove sin, and that it may be the governess of outward discipline. We come to the third question, which is moved by them that deny the divine Laws to be natural, seeing they have so often been changed, with this argument:

> Natural things are unchangeable.  
> The divine Laws have been changed, as against the first table many deities have in all times been feigned, and against the second table many things have been permitted concerning compacts of adulteries, manslaughters, and other things unspeakable.  
> Therefore those Laws are not natural.

First we answer to the argument, denying the minor. For those Laws have not been changed, as touching the knowing part. And as the knowledge of numbers always shineth in the minds of men, so there remaineth the true knowledge, that one God is to be called upon, that there are not many Gods, that a man is not to be slain for sport, that wandering lusts are not to be approved. Hence so many excellent writings of the Heathen concerning the virtues are extant. Hence **Xenophon**, **Aristotle**, **Cicero**, and other wise men disallow a plurality of Gods. ***οὐκ ἀγαθὸν πολυκοιρανίη, εἷς κοίρανος ἔστω*** (the rule of many is not good; let there be one ruler), saith **Aristotle** against the crowd of Gods. And although in this human darkness this light shineth less clearly in the minds of men, yet it cannot utterly be put out. And the wickednesses that are done against the Law do not prove that the Law is not natural, but they show our infirmity and malice, which is to be acknowledged and bewailed; that even against the judgement of reason, which in outward discipline exhorteth unto the best things, seeing it is a ray of the divine wisdom implanted in the mind of man, the will and the heart rush into foul wickednesses, as when the will among the Romans received the slaughters of gladiators, against the judgement of the mind. And the <!-- p.32 --> Lacedaemonians permitted compacts concerning adulteries, and the Germans and others at this day permit usuries and riotous gluttonies. And hereunto is added the cruelty[^P01-5] of the Devils, driving hearts unto horrible furies, in such wise that the darkness also in the knowing part overwhelmeth the judgement. Hence madmen rage against themselves, and against their own. **Hercules** slayeth his wife and his sons.

In the history of the Xanthians a mother hangeth herself from the roof, bearing her son hanged from her neck, and holding a torch wherewith to set the house on fire. And many horrible things doth our own age show. So do the Devils drive nature forsaken of God. Wherefore, being admonished by these tragical examples, whereof scarce any house is void, let us ask governance of the Son of God, who came that he might destroy the works of the Devil, that the light of judgement be not darkened in us, but that it may be polished by his blood, and cleansed. Let us also often think upon and repeat this prayer: *"Lead us not into temptation."* Let it suffice to have admonished these things concerning the divine Law, that it is, and how far it is, naturally known unto us. But the Gospel is not known by nature unto any creature, but was brought forth by the Son of God out of the bosom of the eternal Father, **John 1**, and, as **Isaiah** speaketh, it is a testimony bound up and sealed, that is, not known by nature. Of this property of the Gospel speaketh **Paul**, **1 Cor. 2**, when he saith: *"We speak the wisdom of God in a mystery."* But of these things enough.

The second distinction of the Law and the Gospel is taken from the matter. For the Law is a doctrine enjoining, commanding, accusing, condemning. But the Gospel principally promiseth Remission of sins and life eternal, through and for the sake of Christ, and pronounceth us to be perfect in Christ by the imputation of the perfection of Christ, and by the beginning of new obedience, or the firstfruits of the holy Spirit, but not yet by entire consummation.

The third distinction of the Law and the Gospel is taken from the form of the promises. For the Law hath conditional promises: *"If thou do these things, thou shalt live in them; if thou do them not, thou shalt surely die."* This is the condition of perfect obedience performed, without <!-- p.33 --> pardon, in such wise that even though the sinner repent, yet by the Law he cannot any more be received into favour, but is utterly accursed according to the sentence of the Law, albeit a bare contrition ariseth out of the Law showing sin. But the Gospel hath free promises, that is, it promiseth remission of sins and life eternal freely, without our works and merits, for the sole obedience of Christ, unto all that turn unto Christ, as God saith: *"As I live, I will not the death of a sinner, but that he be converted, and live."* Likewise: *"He that believeth on the Son hath everlasting life."* Than this doctrine there is none more ample, none more pleasant, none more excellent. Unto God be praise and honour. Now there ariseth out of this distinction that kindred one also, That the Law only accuseth and condemneth sin, and all men by reason of sin, and doth not so much as admit any conversion, but simply condemneth the disobedient with eternal death. But that the Gospel freely for Christ's sake giveth unto them that believe remission, and life eternal. These things both ought and may be set forth in a copious discourse. And that this fourth distinction, taken from the form of the threatenings, might be made very plain, God in the promulgation of the Law willed that there should be thunders, lightnings and clouds, a trumpet also exceeding loud and framed to the terror of the whole people, and a thick smoke, signifying that by the voice of the Law man is terrified: but in the promulgation of the Gospel he used a fatherly and sweet voice: *"This is my beloved Son, in whom I wholly rest; hear ye him."* Hence in the Scripture the Law is a flaming sword, forbidding that any of sinners should come unto life, **Genesis 3**. Likewise: hard tables and of stone, to signify the outward and the inward, both obedience and condemnation, **Exod. 34**, **Deut. 6**; a very great fire, **Deut. 18**; the yoke of burden, the staff of the shoulder, the rod of the oppressor, **Isaiah 9**; the band of the neck, **Isaiah 52**; a hammer that breaketh in pieces, **Jerem. 23**; a yoke of iron, **Jeremiah 28**, **Deut. 28**. But the Gospel is the rod of the Kingdom of God, **Ps. 110**; a great Light, **Isaiah 9**; the horn of salvation, the virtue and power of God, <!-- p.34 --> the bread of life, living water, the ministration of the Spirit, not of death, **2 Corinth. 3**, and it offereth pardon unto them that by faith flee unto Christ.

The fifth difference of the Law and the Gospel is taken from the effects. The Law worketh among us wrath, terrors and torments, and is the knowledge of sin, and oftentimes casteth a man into doubtings and despairs. But the Gospel raiseth up minds and consciences, comforteth, healeth, and is the power of God unto salvation to every one that believeth.

The sixth difference is taken from the objects. The Law is to be set forth principally unto the unrighteous, for whom it is made, **1 Timoth. 1**, unto Epicures and hypocrites, the secure and the proud. But the Gospel is set forth unto them that repent, and that tremble with the feeling of the wrath of God against sins, and with the fear of eternal damnation, according to that saying: *"Come unto me, all ye that labour and are heavy laden."*

## *Divisio Legis Divinae* · The Division of the Divine Law

In the holy Scriptures diligent heed is to be given unto the word *Law*, where the Decalogue, where the ceremonies, where the judicial ordinances are understood. Therefore it is usual to say that one Law is Moral, which is the Decalogue; that other Laws are ceremonial; that others, lastly, are judicial (*forenses*). The Moral Law is the Decalogue itself, which God on mount Sinai by his own voice, amid thunders and lightnings, promulgated, in the year of the world 2454. In this year of Christ 1559 there are passed since the giving of the Law 3607 years.[^P01-4]

Here by the way let the words be considered. *Sinai* signifieth a thorn-brake. And the mount is situated in Arabia Petraea, which hath its name from the smelling out or the expounding of dreams and riddles. For truly the Law, the tables whereof are of stone, setteth riddles before us, and containeth the figures and types of the Gospel, and cannot bring us unto true waking, or life, but overwhelmeth us with heavy sleep and terrible dreams. All these things pertain to the vehemency of the Law.

<!-- p.35 -->

Many godly men also reason that the thunders, which are wont still to be, are admonitions both of the past and of the future: of the past, namely, the Law that was given; of the future, the last judgement, wherein the Son of God shall come to judge the quick and the dead. But *Sion* signifieth a watch-tower or a sign, *ein Schawbergk* (a look-out hill): the mount of the city of Jerusalem, upon which the stronghold of David was placed, of which it is said: *"Out of Sion shall the Law go forth."* Hither are the promises of the Gospel referred. And the explication of these words illustrateth the distinction of the Law and the Gospel.

The Moral Law therefore, or the Decalogue, is the eternal and unmoved wisdom and rule of righteousness in God, discerning things right and not right, and horribly angry with the stubborn, revealed unto men in the Creation, and afterward often repeated, and declared by the divine voice, that we may know that God is, and of what manner he is; binding all reasonable creatures, either unto obedience, or unto punishment or damnation, unless there be made remission and reconciliation for the sake of Christ the Mediator.

The ceremonial Laws ordered the outward gestures in the sacrifices, and distinguished places, times and persons, and described certain other outward exercises, that they might be signs and distinctions of the Mosaical polity from other nations, *Kirchenordnung* (a church order).

The judicial Laws are the Laws concerning judgements and magistrates, concerning outward discipline, concerning punishments, and the whole political order.

## *De abrogatione Legis Mosaicae* · Of the Abrogation of the Mosaical Law

I. As touching justification, all the parts of the Law avail nothing, **Rom. 3**: *"By the works of the Law shall no man be justified."*

II. As touching obedience, only the ceremonial and judicial Laws are abolished: but the Moral Law remaineth unmoved, **Rom. 3**: *"Through faith we establish the Law."* And the Son of God himself saith: *"I am not come to destroy the Law."*

<!-- p.36 -->

III. As touching condemnation, the curse of the Decalogue is abolished in them that believe, according to the saying: *"There is now no condemnation to them which walk in Christ Jesus."* Therefore it is wont commonly to be said: We are no more under the Law, although we be in the Law; that is, the Law doth not condemn us who believe on the Son of God, although we owe obedience unto it.

**But it is asked, why the ceremonial and judicial Laws, as touching the species (for the Genus always remaineth), may be utterly omitted?** We answer, Because they were promulgated only for a certain time, so long as the Mosaical polity should endure. The polity therefore being fallen, it was necessary that the Laws also should cease. For the Mosaical polity was ordained for this cause, that there might be a certain place and a certain people, wherein the Messiah should be born. When therefore the Messiah was born, the polity perished, according to the prophecy: *"The sceptre shall not be taken away from Judah, until Shiloh come,"* the promised Messiah.

## *De usu Legis Moralis* · Of the Use of the Moral Law

**Seeing the Moral Law cannot be blotted out, and yet cannot be fulfilled by us, it is asked, what is the use thereof?**

**I answer.** The use is threefold. The first is universal, which for teaching's sake is named the Pedagogical or Political use (*usus Paedagogicus seu Politicus*): namely, to restrain the outward members in all men, or the locomotive power, that the outward deeds may agree with the Moral Law, and discipline may be preserved, according to the rules: *"He that taketh the sword shall perish with the sword."* *"Woe to thee that spoilest, for thou shalt be spoiled."* *"Whoremongers and adulterers God will judge."* **1 Timoth. 1**: *"The Law is made for the unrighteous."* Likewise: Heinous offences are punished with heinous punishments, as experience in the whole race of mankind, and all histories, do testify. Hence among the Heathen there are very notable Legal sentences:

> Learn righteousness, being warned, and not to despise  
> the Gods, etc.  

<!-- p.37 -->

And this use of the Law is named by a general word, Discipline, the profits whereof it very greatly concerneth us to remember. First, it is necessary to render discipline, because of the most earnest commandment of God, unto which obedience is due. Next, to avoid the punishments wherewith either the magistrate or God punisheth heinous offences. Afterward, for the sake of public peace and tranquillity, that men may be governed and taught. Fourthly, because discipline is a pedagogy unto Christ, that is, in them Christ is not effectual, who cease not to defile themselves with wickednesses against conscience. This praise of discipline is great, and unknown to the Heathen. And these things may be unfolded in a notable discourse.

The second use of the Law is the Theological (*usus Theologicus*): to show and reprove sin, to strike in terror, to make manifest infirmity and the wrath of God. Hence **Paul** saith: *"The Law worketh wrath."* *"The sting of death is sin, etc."* *"By the Law is the knowledge of sin."* *"By the Law sin becometh exceeding guilty."* This use of the Law is felt in true repentance, not in secure men, as **Paul** saith: *"I was alive without the Law once,"* that is, I was secure, I felt not the judgement of God; afterward, being thoroughly terrified, I acknowledged my sins. But always unto this use of the Law there is to be added the voice of the Gospel, showing the Lamb of God, which taketh away sins. For we are terrified by the Law, not that we should perish, but that we should flee unto the Mediator, as **Paul** saith: *"He hath concluded all under sin, that he might have mercy upon all."* And this use of the Law chiefly showeth that men are not justified by the Law, but accused.
