The latter question is: **How ought the godly to bear themselves in calamities?** <!-- p.74 --> I answer: with Patience. Of this, namely, there is need, which alone overcometh Satan and confoundeth the world. Now this Patience cometh by the acknowledgment and invocation of God, as well in prosperity as in adversity. Therefore **Bernard** saith: *"He that directeth his heart unto the wounds of Christ, who is the rock, feeleth not his own wounds."* And **Vincent** to the tyrant: *"Put forth thy fury; thou shalt see me stronger to suffer than thou art to afflict."* For Christians are serpents, which in the conflict guard their head; that is, they let not Christ, Emmanuel and ***παραστάτην*** (Him that standeth by to help), go out of their hearts, as the Son of God Himself thus saith: *"Be ye wise as serpents."*

There are also the heads of the chief consolations to be held fast. **I.** It is to be known that we are afflicted by the counsel of God, God knowing and permitting it, not by chance, nor by matter so stirring it. **II.** A good conscience, if it be present. **III.** Faith embracing the promised reconciliation for Christ's sake. **IIII.** The examples of Abel, of the Son of God, of the Baptist, of the Martyrs, as it is written: *"I am not better than my fathers."* **V.** The consideration of the final causes, whereof the first is the exercise of the fear of God, of faith, of invocation: The second is, that the wrath of God against sin may be acknowledged, in the Church first of all, and that evils may be amended: The third is, that the afflictions of the godly may be testimonies of doctrine and of immortality: The fourth is, that the Saints may be made like unto the image of the Son of God: The fifth, that the help and presence of God may be seen, as Job saith: *"Though he slay me, yet will I trust in him."* These things are more copiously unfolded elsewhere.

### *De Perseverantia* · Of Perseverance

Lastly, unto the fear of God, and unto humility and patience, there cleaveth also perseverance in the true acknowledgment of God and in faith in Christ, or in the truth acknowledged; of which perseverance it is said, **Matt. 24**: *"He that shall endure unto the end, the same shall be saved."* And **Rev. 2**: *"Be thou faithful unto death, and I will give <!-- p.75 --> thee a crown of life."* Likewise **John 10**: *"No man shall pluck my sheep out of my hands."* **Matt. 16**: *"The gates of hell shall not prevail against the Church."* For the Spirit of truth abideth with us for ever, **John 14**.

This perseverance in prosperity falleth not away from God, neither feigneth anything new and strange from the word of God, but defendeth and retaineth the true doctrine: in adversity it murmureth not against God, but endureth evils; in persecutions it casteth not away courage, but is constant, and uttereth its confession with a stout mind even unto death. Here let the saying of Solomon be observed: *"The path of the just goeth forth as a shining light, and increaseth unto the perfect day"*; that is, it is constant, until it be brought through unto the heavenly kingdom.

## *De Peccatis contra I. Praeceptum* · Of the Sins against the First Commandment

**I.** It is asked, **Whether men satisfy the first Commandment, which requireth perfect obedience, according to that: *"Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thy heart"*?** I answer: Our weak nature can never satisfy the Law; we never fear or love God so much as was meet. There cleaveth in us doubting, security, distrust, concupiscence, etc. For the nature of man is corrupted by the fault of our origin. *"There is none righteous, no, not one."*

**II.** **Can there not be begun some obedience of this Commandment?** I answer: Unless the Gospel concerning Christ and concerning the Remission of sins be heard, no obedience can be begun. For human hearts cannot believe God, they do not feel that they are heard, they do not love God, unless they first understand that God is appeased through and for the sake of the Son, who was made a sacrifice for us. Whence it is said: *"Christ is the end of the Law."* Likewise: *"The Law is established through faith,"* etc. For when we hear that God is propitious unto us through the Son, we are filled with sure consolation, and begin to believe and to trust in GOD, we <!-- p.76 --> acknowledge His mercy, we hold that we are regarded and heard, we love and fear God, and live in continual conversion, and begin an obedience which pleaseth God by faith for Christ's sake.

**III.** **But what are the sins against the first Commandment?** Before we speak of the sins in particular, it is necessary to hold the general doctrine concerning sin, which is thus defined, **1 John 3**: ***ἡ ἁμαρτία ἐστὶν ἀνομία***, sin is whatsoever agreeth not with the rule of the divine Law, that is, whatsoever is done: **I.** Against the Law. **II.** And without the Law; as feigned and Pharisaical works are done without the Law: they are not those works which God hath commanded, but are vain worships and commandments of men, wherewith God is worshipped in vain. Now this definition is delivered: Sin is a defect, or an inclination (these genera agree to the evil of our origin), or an action (comprehending all actual sins, inward and outward), fighting with the Law of God (the common difference, **Rom. 7**), offending God, condemned by God, and making men guilty of eternal wrath and of eternal punishments (these are the properties), unless remission be made for the sake of the Mediator, the Son of God.

Here first it is asked, **Whence did sin arise?** Namely, from the Devil, who sinneth from the beginning, and from the free will of our first parents. For the Devil abode not in the truth. Likewise: *"When the Devil speaketh a lie, he speaketh of his own. For he is a liar, and a murderer from the beginning."* Likewise: *"He that committeth sin is of the Devil."* And **Ps. 5**: *"Thou art not a God that hath pleasure in wickedness."* God neither willeth, nor worketh, nor approveth, nor helpeth sin; which thing the sounder Heathen also, **Plato**, **Homer**, **Pindar** and others, have often expressed in most excellent sentences.

And here the evil of guilt (*malum culpae*) is to be discerned from the evil of punishment (*malum poenae*), of which it is said: *"There is no evil in the city, which the Lord hath not done."* Likewise: *"The Lord hath made even the wicked for the day of judgement."* And thus of the evil of punishment is understood in the Church this phrase, that God punisheth sins with sins; as the Turk is the scourge <!-- p.77 --> of God, such as Tamerlane also said that he was, King of the Parthians, in the year 1398, who seized upon Asia the less with a huge army, of 40,000 mailed horsemen and 60,000 footmen, having overcome Bajazet, Emperor of the Turks; whom, being taken, Tamerlane bound with chains of gold, and, having thrust him into an iron cage, carried about through all Asia and Syria, and, whensoever he mounted his horse, used him for a footstool.

Now I will add this by the way. Tamerlane, when he had besieged any Town, on the first day pitched tents of a white colour, signifying grace and kindness; on the second day, tents of a red colour, signifying blood and slaughter; on the third day he set up black tents, denoting the laying waste of the Town. It happened upon a time that a certain Town put off its surrender unto the second day, on which there were sent unto Tamerlane women and maidens, with others, infants, wearing white garments, and bearing the olive in their hands, in token of a peaceable surrender. But because the tents of red colour were already pitched, no kindness was shown, all being slain and thrust through. Tamerlane, being asked by one of his Counsellors with what mind he exercised this tyranny, answered: *"Thinkest thou that I am a man? I am the wrath of God, and the laying waste of the earth. I counsel thee that hereafter thou speak not unto me of this matter; otherwise thou shalt feel my cruelty upon thine own self."* In this year of Christ 1559 there are passed since then 161 years. So also the tyrant Attila inscribed himself *The Scourge of God, and the terror of the peoples*, in the year of Christ 451. He laid waste Rome, a great part of Germany, the Gauls, etc. So at this day the Pope is the punishment of security and ungodliness. The Turk likewise, and other enemies. And privately also, in families, sins are often punished with sins by the law of retaliation. As is the offence, such is the punishment. He that sinneth in his seed is punished in his seed. The fraudulent are deceived by frauds. Disobedient children beget stubborn children.

Here let the **Stoics** also be refuted, who cast the cause of all evils upon God, and feign a necessity <!-- p.78 --> in all things natural and voluntary.

Moreover, the kinds of sin are the sin of origin (*peccatum originis*) and actual sin (*peccatum actuale*).

Original sin is, in all men born of the seed of man, the loss of light in the mind, the turning away of the will from God, and the stubbornness of the heart against the Law of God, born with us, and following upon the fall of Adam; by reason of which corruption of nature men are guilty of the wrath of God and of eternal punishments, unless remission be made for the sake of the Mediator, the Son of God.

**Rom. 5**: *"By the offence of one all are dead. Death passed upon all, for that all have sinned,"* that is, are guilty, and have sin, a thing evil and condemned, and the evil is propagated unto all.

**Rom. 8**: *"The mind of the flesh is enmity against God: for it is not subject to the Law of God, neither indeed can be."*

**Eph. 2**: *"We were by nature the children of wrath,"* that is, guilty and condemned. **Ps. 50 [51]**: *"Behold, I was shapen in iniquity; and in sin did my mother conceive me."*

**Gen. 8**: *"The imagination of man's heart is evil from his youth."* *[Margin: **כָּל יֵצֶר** (kol yetser, every imagination)]* Now it is named *Iezar*, a thought, ***πλάσμα***, a workmanship, a thing formed, a fashioning, the lump of the heart, all its ***ὁρμαί***, or impulses. **Job 9**: *"I know of a truth that a man is not just before God."* **Jer. 17**: *"The heart of men is perverse above all things"* (that is, it is turned away from God, and is an enemy unto God) *"and wretched and unsearchable,"* or full of sorrows, full of trouble and ulcerated (these are the punishments of the defects). The greatness of this evil cannot be sufficiently understood by us.

**Ps. 116**: *"I said in my transport,"* that is, in my consternation, or my dread: *"All men are liars."*

Now it is asked, **Whether after Baptism it be rightly said that sin is in infants, or that concupiscence is sin?** I answer: In Baptism sin is taken away as touching the guilt, or the imputation; but the disease itself remaineth. The formal is taken away, the material remaineth (*tollitur formale, manet materiale*), as <!-- p.79 --> **Paul** clearly saith: *"In the members is a Law warring against the Law of the mind,"* and against the Law of God. Let those things also which are works of God remaining in man be discerned from the things which are of themselves vicious; as the knowledge of numbers is a good thing, because it was implanted in man by God. So also of the affections some are good and commanded of God, as the love of offspring, of spouses, of parents; others are forbidden by the Law of God.

Let the argument of **Julian** concerning Concupiscence be refuted:

> Concupiscence begetteth.  
> Concupiscence is sin.  
> Therefore sin begetteth.

I deny it of the ***ἀταξία*** (disorder) of nature. Concupiscence doth not beget, but the order which remaineth in nature, established by God; but that Concupiscence cometh in besides is ***ἀταξία***, and cometh to pass by accident.

Let there be refuted also the raving of the more recent Manichees concerning sin, that it is the very substance of man, created by the Devil; which is horrible to be heard, foul, and plainly contumelious against Christ, who assuredly did not assume our nature as a thing formed by the Devil, and who is Himself our creator. For the Devil can create nothing out of nothing.

Let the Monks also be refuted, who said that the appetites which are good, and created by God in man, are the material of original sin, and said that this remaineth after Baptism, but that the defect is taken away. They understood not what they said. They named Concupiscence the senses themselves, or the natural appetite, whereas they ought to have understood it to be the ***ἀταξίαν*** (disorder) of the appetites, and withal to have comprehended the darkness of the mind and the evils of the will. Thus much of original sin.

Actual sin is an action, either inward or outward, fighting with the Law of God. This ariseth out of the former.

Now here a distinction is to be made between the unregenerate and the regenerate. All sins in the unregenerate, original and actual, are mortal. For this Rule abideth unmoved: *"He that believeth not on the Son, the wrath of God abideth on him."* Here no virtues avail without the acknowledgment of the Son. But the sins in them that are reconciled — as the original evil, and many inward actual sins, fighting with the Law of God, which yet the regenerate resist, and many sins of ignorance <!-- p.80 --> and of omission — are venial, that is, the guilt is remitted unto them that retain faith and by the Spirit repress the depraved affections.

Next, the sins which remain in the regenerate in this life are to be discerned from those by reason of which grace, the Holy Spirit and faith are lost. There remain in the saints sins, the original, and many inward actual sins, but not against conscience. But when the elect rush on against conscience, there are made mortal sins, that is, grace, the Holy Spirit and faith are lost, unless there be made anew a conversion unto God, and remission for the Mediator's sake, as in Adam and Eve, in Aaron, David, Peter and many others. Neither is judgement to be made concerning sin according to election, which we perversely endeavour to search out, but according to the word of God delivered unto us. Adam and Eve were elect, and yet they did in very deed lose the Holy Spirit in the fall, being turned away from God, and were made guilty of eternal wrath. And of Aaron it is said, **Deut. 9**: *"The Lord was very angry with Aaron to have destroyed him; but Moses prayed for him."* These most sorrowful words of the Holy Spirit are not to be eluded. And **Paul**, **Rom. 8**, delivereth the difference: *"If ye live after the flesh, ye shall die: but if ye through the Spirit do mortify the deeds of the flesh, ye shall live"*; that is, in the saints there are many vicious inclinations, which must be resisted by spiritual motions, by the invocation of God, by fear, by faith, by a spiritual endurance that looketh unto God, and by a chastity that looketh unto God. And the regenerate abide in grace, although those vicious affections be in them, if yet they resist, and by faith acknowledge the forgiveness for Christ's sake; but if they resist not, Paul affirmeth that they shall die.

Now that it may be understood who they are that resist not sins, he setteth down a degree, namely, them that offend against conscience, that is, those who knowingly and willingly indulge those vicious motions and flames, or even obey them in outward action. And that which is here named sin against conscience is named elsewhere reigning or ruling sin (*peccatum regnans seu dominans*), of which **Paul**, **Rom. 6**: *"Let not sin reign in your <!-- p.81 --> mortal body."* And **Ps. 118 [119]**: *"Order my steps, and let not any iniquity have dominion over me."* And the name seemeth to be taken from the 4th chapter of **Genesis**: *"Let thy appetite be under thee, and rule thou over it."* Now sin hath dominion, when the guilt is not remitted, but kindleth the wrath of God, and plucketh man away from God, and man, being forsaken of God, is driven by his own infirmity and by the Devil, so that he rusheth out of some evils into others, and heapeth up wicked deeds and punishments, as Saul, Cain, Judas, Ahab, Oedipus, Atreus, Thyestes, Nero, and other plagues of mankind. Whence it is said in the verses:

> ***ὅταν γὰρ ὀργὴ δαιμόνων βλάπτῃ τινὰ,***  
> ***τοῦτ᾽ αὐτὸ πρῶτον ἐξαφαιρεῖται φρενῶν***  
> ***τὸν νοῦν τὸν ἐσθλὸν, εἰς δὲ τὴν χείρω τρέπει***  
> ***γνώμην ἵν᾽ εἰδῇ μηδὲν ὧν ἁμαρτάνει.***  

> When God in anger draweth any unto punishment,  
> He is wont first to take away the mind from such,  
> And poureth darkness over them, that they may rush  
> In fury into their own ruins, which with hurtful  
> Evil counsels they have of their own accord brought upon themselves.  

[^P03-1]: The print reads *quo die Deus antea dilexit* ("on which day God before loved"); *die* is taken as a misprint for *te*.

[^P03-2]: So the print (*actuali et effectuali*). Bernard's sermon distinguishes charity in act and charity in affection (*in actu* and *in affectu*).

Now there is made in the sacred Scriptures, **Matt. 12** and **1 John 5**, mention of a peculiar sin, which is against the Holy Ghost, and unto death. This sin **Augustine** understandeth of final impenitence and despair, which denieth and refuseth the grace of God. And this judgement of Augustine is true and godly, and judgeth of this sin *a posteriori*, that is, it ascribeth the sin unto death to those who, departing out of this life, despise the Son of God and grace, or remain finally impenitent, as Pharaoh, Saul, Judas. And in the *Sentences*, **book 2, dist. 43**, it is rightly said: that the sin against the Holy Ghost is committed by the final contempt of the divine goodness and grace. These things concerning sins in general, which are reproved in the Law of God, it hath seemed good to set down beforehand.

Now we must speak of the sins against the first Commandment. With faith these sins are at strife: **I.** Epicurean and Academic doubtings. **II.** Distrust (*diffidentia*). **III.** Despair. <!-- p.82 --> Of these we have spoken above. And these are sins ***κατ᾽ ἔκλειψιν ἢ ἔνδειαν*** (by failing or want). But ***κατὰ δὲ ὑπερβολὴν*** (by excess) are these: **IIII.** Confidence or presumption, trusting in a man's own powers, wisdom and learning, as Ajax, Nebuchadnezzar. **V.** Trust in human defences, which is censured in **Jer. 17** and **Isa. 8**. Idolatry, which consisteth in these four kinds: **I.** To feign that something is God which is not God, as to worship statues, the worship whereof is reported to have taken its beginning from Ninus the son of Belus, in Assyria. For after the death of his father Belus, Ninus set up a great image with great privileges, that they who should adore and worship it might enjoy the same. Hence there was made from time to time a great resort thither. And the sport of the Devil more and more imposed upon men, even as in our own age, in the Papacy, there was a great resort to statues, to the Mary of Regensburg, and elsewhere. **Lyra** thinketh that hence are the names Bel, Baal, Beelzebub. Now mention is often made in the Scriptures of Baal, which word signifieth *Lord*. Whence are *Baalpeor*, the God of the Moabites, so called from opening, whom the heathen, as it seemeth, the name being a little changed, called Priapus, and who, as **Matthesius** thinketh, was so called as being the father of fruitfulness. *Beelzebub*, the prince or lord of flies, the god of the Ekronites, to whom King Ahaziah sent, inquiring whether he should recover of his sickness, **2 Kings 1**. *Baalberith*, a God of the heathen, whom the people of Israel worshipped after Gideon was dead, **Judg. 8**. It is exceeding marvellous that all these Idols were worshipped by the Jews. So easily doth the wit of man slide away from the true God. But dreadful punishments from God laid hold on this Idolatry in the people of Israel, **2 Kings 1**, **3 Kings 18 [1 Kings 18]**. The heathen worshipped many images of this sort. The Babylonians and Assyrians, Belus: the Egyptians, Osiris and Isis: the Africans, Neptune: the Moors, Juba: the Rhodians and Massagetae, the Sun: Samos, Juno: Lemnos, Vulcan: Paphos, Venus: Delphi, Apollo: Rome, Quirinus: Athens, Minerva. And **Terentius Varro** writeth that the heathen had three hundred Jupiters. And **Oenomaus** and **D. Augustine** write that 30000 idols were adored by the heathen. These things are horrible, and therefore the <!-- p.83 --> more horrible, because even in the assembly of Christians and in the Papacy, in our own age, the same idols, and often more, have everywhere been worshipped. To God be praise and glory, who hath brought back again the light of the Gospel. To this first kind belong they also who turned into Gods the outward[^P04-1] signs which were divinely added to the promise of the Gospel, as the Chaldeans and Persians feigned fire to be God, when they had heard that the sacrifices were kindled by fire fallen from heaven, as is said in **Gen. 11**. And the Turks also in like manner call upon a God who is not God. For the creation of things is common and proper to the three persons, whom these men acknowledge not. Thus many divine worships were, by a dreadful ***κακοζηλίᾳ*** (perverse imitation), given to strange gods, as the promise of the seed was referred to Priapus, **Numbers 25**: *"The people began to commit whoredom with the Moabites, and was joined unto Baalpeor, and Jehovah was kindled against Israel."* The promise of the sacrifice, or of the Son of God to be slain for mankind, was referred to Moloch, to whom parents offered their children to be burnt, which idol Solomon also served, **3 Kings 11 [1 Kings 11]**. Now Moloch was a hollow image with seven chambers: in one a door was opened for fine flour to be offered, the second for turtle-doves, the third for a sheep, the fourth for a ram, the fifth for a calf, the sixth for an ox, the seventh for a son. The face of this idol was as the face of a calf, the hands flat, and disposed to receive from them that stood by: and they danced the while,[^P04-2] whilst the child was burnt in the idol with the fire kindled beneath, beating Timbrels, that the parents might not hear the wailing of the infants. Hence also the word *Tophet* arose. For *Toph* signifieth a Timbrel. The place was in the valley of Hinnom, whence *Gehinnon*, that is, the valley of Hinnom, and *Gehenna*, a place appointed for the dead, **Isaiah 30**; likewise, hell.

The second kind of idolatry is to give divine honour to things or creatures of whatsoever sort, as the covetous man is an idolater.

The third kind is to bind God to things which He hath not set forth by His own word, as the adoration of Statues, in which Christ or the Saints are thought to be found. Whence pilgrimages hither and thither arose. For although the use of godly Statues, for <!-- p.84 --> remembrance' sake, in a Church rightly ordered, neither can nor ought to be forbidden, yet God is not to be bound to them.

The fourth kind is to choose worships without the word of God, and to trust in them, etc.

Furthermore, Magic is at strife with faith, that is, as we use this word at this day, a covenant made with the Devil, of what sort soever and concerning what thing soever. Now it is to be known that the word *Magia* is Persian, perhaps from the Hebrew *Mahaegim*, that is, Meditators or Philosophers, *die Gelehrten* (the learned). And **Plato** writeth in the *Alcibiades* that the Persian Kings delivered a son, when he was fourteen years old, to be instructed by four Magi, most excellent men: namely, the wisest, who should teach the doctrine concerning God and the divine worships; next, the most just, who should faithfully show the things that pertain to the government of the Commonwealth; afterward, the most temperate, who should frame his manners and private life; lastly, the most valiant, who should set forth the arts of war, and render the Prince great of soul. This was a discipline not like to our courts, in which Princes are for the most part made companions of ***στρατιώταις*** (soldiers), from whom they learn blasphemies, surfeitings, and the contempt of honest things. But since both the name and the thing have come into abuse, that is now called Magic which is an ungodly covenant struck with Devils, auguries, magical lots, *pythons*, that is, Spirits which dwell in frantic persons, and being asked do prophesy.

In the sacred Scriptures there are extant the names of these kinds.

**I.** *Ob*, **1 Sam. 28**, out of the history of King Saul. The woman, consulting Demons, prophesieth, *Ob* being consulted, or a python answering them that consult. Now *Ob* signifieth a skin-bottle or flagon, from the swelling of them that are possessed; in Greek ***ἐγγαστρίμυθον*** (one that speaketh from the belly), from ***γαστὴρ***, that is, the belly, as it were a vessel blown up. And such, for the most part, are they who at this time consult the Devil in a crystal, images being shown therein.

**II.** *Iedeuni*, that is, to know, to foretell, to have knowledge of things to come, to be enchanted, when others by certain words and images are bewitched, <!-- p.85 --> hurt, or deceived, as when wasting ulcers are wrought in others by enchantment; even as it is read in the life of **Macarius**, that there was a maiden who, through the spite of her lover, to whom she had refused wantonness, was by an ungodly Jew changed into the form of a horse, so that she was taken by all for a horse. This enchantment was reported to Macarius, who commanded the maiden to be brought unto him. For the holy man could not in this matter be deceived by the Devil, as other secure men were: but Macarius prayed unto God, and so the maiden was delivered from her enchantment; whom he admonished that thenceforth she should be godly, and should often hear the word of God, and that then there would be no more danger from the evil. And **Hermes** saith godly: *"The one safeguard is godliness. For a godly man neither an evil Demon nor fate holdeth, for God delivereth the godly from all evil."* Now in this manner women have often been transformed into men, and men into women. And such without doubt are the ***ἀρκτόλυκοι*** (bear-wolves), whom we name *Beerwolff* (werewolves).

**III.** *Meonem*, they who superstitiously choose and shun certain days, so called from cloud or vapour, as in the ***ἡμέραις*** (*Days*) of **Hesiod**. Likewise: they who fear Saturn's night, or choose Jove's day for the searching out of secret things, which are done everywhere.

**IIII.** *Menaches*, soothsayers by lot, who divine by figures, or numbers, or lots.

**V.** *Micaseph*, a Sorcerer, who by Devilish juggleries bringeth something to pass, and blindeth men, so that nothing is seen, or that a thing is seen to be such as it is not; as that virgin at Bologna, who conversed as though alive with her parents and others, and was brought to dances that she might play upon the cithern, whereas she was yet a dead body; which thing another sorcerer afterward detected, having taken away the charm which she carried under her arms. Such are the works of the Egyptian Magicians, Jannes and Jambres, who wrought the appearance of serpents.

**VI.** *Hober*, a companion. The abuse of the doctrine of the stars, especially in particulars.

These and more kinds are all accursed, and in the sacred <!-- p.86 --> Scriptures are most severely forbidden of God, especially in the twentieth of **Leviticus**: *"A man or a woman, in whom there is a Pythonic spirit, or enchantment, let them be put to death."*

Let these things suffice in this place concerning the sins at strife with faith.

With love are at strife, in defect: the hatred of God, murmuring and indignation against the judgement of God, or impatience in afflictions; the dullness of hearts in the secure, who feel not the burning flames of love. But in ***ὑπερβολῇ*** (excess): ***ἀταξία*** (disorder) of love, of gladness, of the appetites and powers in man.

With the fear of God is at strife, in defect: security, or to be without care, and without faith and love, as is the greatest part of mankind; the Epicurean and Cyclopean contempt of God. In ***ὑπερβολῇ*** (excess): superstition, trembling with a fear not filial but servile, when there ought to be no fear there, as hypocrites fearing lest they profane Friday and Saturday when they eat flesh. Now superstition is to ascribe some power to anything whatsoever without divine ordinance, and without natural causes, and without the experience of men of sound mind, as to wear about the neck certain figures, which should heal us.

With humility are at strife feigned humility, **Col. 2**, and pride, of which it hath been spoken above.

With patience are at strife impatience in afflictions, and the hypocritical endurance of torments drawn upon oneself of one's own accord, as in the Flagellants[^P04-3] and Hermits.

With perseverance are at strife ***ἀποστασία*** (apostasy), or falling away from the truth once acknowledged; and stubbornness in opinions, as in Servetus, the Anabaptists, and the Sacramentarians. And these are the sins which are at strife with the first Commandment.

### *Quae autem est poena violati primi Praecepti* · But What Is the Punishment of the First Commandment Broken

The breaking of the Decalogue is punished with eternal death, **Matt. 5**: <!-- p.87 --> *"Except your righteousness shall exceed."* Likewise: *"If thou wilt enter into life, keep the commandments."*

**II.** It is punished with temporal punishments. And to the first Commandment there is added a peculiar threatening, punishing unto the third and fourth generation. For this threatening is understood of temporal things, as it is also said in the verse:

> ***εὐσεβέων παίδεσσι τὰ λώϊα, δυσσεβέων δ᾽ οὔ.***  
> (To the children of the godly the better things, but to those of the ungodly, not.)

And in the **Psalm**: *"The generation of the upright shall be blessed."* And contrariwise: The generation of the ungodly shall be cursed, as the examples show. **1 Kings 40**:[^P04-4] For the idolatry of Solomon the kingdom of Israel was rent asunder, and there followed a perpetual discord of the two kingdoms. And **2 Kings 24 [2 Sam. 24]**, when David, against the commandment of God, had numbered the people, the choice of three punishments was set before him, of war, pestilence and famine. And the King, seeing that it was more tolerable to fall into the hands of the Lord than of enemies, chose the pestilence, and there fell within three days seventy thousand men. Likewise, **Judg. 20**, for the Levite's wife, forced and slain, two and twenty thousand Israelites were slaughtered in one day. In **Num. 25** it is shown that four and twenty thousand men fell for fornication, which history Paul allegeth in **1 Cor. 10**. For the sins of Manasseh the whole tribe of Judah is rooted out. For the sin of Achan his Sons also and his wife, and all that he had, are burnt, **Josh. 7**. In **3 Kings 11 [1 Kings 11]** God saith: *"I will rend the kingdom from thee, and will give it to thy servant. Notwithstanding in thy days I will not do it, for David thy Father's sake: but I will rend it out of the hand of thy Son."* **3 Kings 14 and 15 [1 Kings 14 and 15]**: *"The Lord shall give Israel up unto a curse, because of the sins of Jeroboam, who did sin, and who made Israel to sin."* **3 Kings 16 [1 Kings 16]**: *"I will root out the posterity of Baasha."* **4 Kings 8 [2 Kings 8]**: *"The Lord would not destroy Judah, for David his servant's sake."* And these examples often meet us, even in daily experience, as it is wont to be said:

> In goods ill gotten the third heir hath no joy.  

And **Solomon**: *"Oftentimes a whole city payeth for the evil of one man."* And **Hesiod**:

<!-- p.88 -->

> ***πολλάκι καὶ ξύμπασα πόλις κακοῦ ἀνδρὸς ἐπαυρεῖ,***  
> ***ὅστις ἀλιτραίνει, καὶ ἀτάσθαλα μηχανάαται,***  
> (Oftentimes even a whole city reapeth the fruit of an evil man,  
> who sinneth, and deviseth presumptuous things,)  

Hence it may be seen that God punisheth the line of the seed which hath sinned: and often spareth the man who hath sinned, and turneth the punishment upon his posterity, as in **3 Kings 21 [1 Kings 21]** He saith unto Elijah: *"Seest thou how Ahab is humbled before me? because therefore he is humbled for my sake, I will not bring the evil in his days: but in his son's days will I bring the evil upon his house."*

God often punisheth whole kingdoms for the offences of the magistrate, or of certain other men, as the history of the daughters of Scedasus showeth, and our own age aboundeth with examples.

These things are marvellous, but too true. But let us learn to live in the fear of God, who spareth His own, and mitigateth punishments. Let us know this also, that God forgiveth sins unto them that repent, and that temporal punishments are mitigated, and that no man is punished with eternal punishment save for his own sin, as the voice of the Gospel, which is here to be brought unto the threatenings of the Law, manifestly teacheth. From these things this objection ought to be resolved: *"The son shall not bear the iniquity of the father"*, namely, in eternal punishments, and in civil judgement. For temporal punishments from God range through many. But the Gospel, as hath now been said, delivereth an exception to this sentence of the Law, as Ezekiel teacheth concerning the Gospel. For they are excepted whom the Gospel excepteth. The ungodly are not excepted. This true doctrine is wholesome to the godly, to the ungodly dreadful and terrible.

### *Quae est promissio primi praecepti?* · What Is the Promise of the First Commandment?

**I.** Life eternal.

**II.** Temporal rewards.

For godliness is profitable unto all things, having promise of the life that now is, and of that which is to come. And the promise is diligently to be considered: *"Shewing mercy unto thousands of them that love me."* For it showeth withal that the mercy of God is greater <!-- p.89 --> than the wrath of God, even the just wrath. For in the threatening mention is made only of the third and fourth generation. But here, in the promise, it is spoken of many thousands. So much doth the grace of God abound above sin, as Paul testifieth.

But seeing that the promises are conditional, and that the Law is not satisfied, what is to be held concerning good works, and concerning their rewards, hath been sufficiently said above. Works please God, even though imperfect in us, and they please for Christ's sake, and have their rewards in this life and in that which is to come, as Christ clearly saith: *"Great is your reward in heaven."* Likewise: *"Them that honour me I will honour, etc."*

# *Secundum Praeceptum* · The Second Commandment

The first Commandment teacheth of the inward worship of the mind. The Second, of the outward signification, which is made by invocation and doctrine. This order is most fitting.

### *Cultus secundi praecepti sunt* · The Worships of the Second Commandment Are

**I.** The true invocation of God.

**II.** True thanksgiving.

**III.** True preaching (*praedicatio*).

**IIII.** Confession.

**V.** The oath.

### *De invocatione Dei, et gratiarum actione* · Of the Invocation of God, and of Thanksgiving

Of these two worships we must speak in the exposition of the Lord's Prayer. But let true invocation be distinguished from the invocation of the Jews and Turks, who are ignorant both of the essence and of the will of God.

<!-- p.90 -->

Unto thanksgiving for benefits spiritual and bodily let these move us: **I.** The commandments of God, who requireth thankfulness of us, as He Himself saith: *"Call upon me in the day of trouble: and I will deliver thee, and thou shalt glorify me."* **II.** Because God is most of all delighted with thanksgiving, as **Psalm 116** testifieth: *"What shall I render unto the Lord for all his benefits toward me? I will take the cup of salvation, and will proclaim the name of the Lord."* **III.** Because in this manner we win God unto ourselves, and, as it were taking hold of Him beforehand, possess Him, so that His benefits continually increase. For *"whosoever hath"*, that is, whosoever acknowledgeth that he hath from God, *"to him shall be given."* **IIII.** Because, if there be no thanksgiving, even that which is had is taken away, as from Jeroboam, Saul, and others. **V.** Thanksgiving keepeth every man in true humility. For he that giveth thanks declareth that all good things are from God. The proud give not thanks, as Nebuchadnezzar, ascribing all things to his own power. **VI.** Thanksgiving serveth to this, that every man live content with those things which he hath received from God.

### *De vera praedicatione* · Of True Preaching

True preaching looketh upon the essence and will of God. This is laid open in the Law, and in the Gospel. **Plato** spake thus: God, fearing lest the whole race of men should perish, gave unto men ***αἰδῶ τε καὶ δίκην***, shame and justice. These things indeed the Divines have referred to the Law and the Gospel. For the Law worketh shame, showing sins, so that the sinner dareth not lift up his eyes unto heaven. But the Gospel raiseth up, and pointeth out righteousness. By these two mankind is sustained. But these things, not understood by Plato, let us leave.

True preaching concerning the essence and will of God requireth three things: **I.** The knowledge of the doctrine concerning God. **II.** Constancy. **III.** Diligence in all the parts of doctrine: in doctrine, in the use of the Sacraments, in examples of godliness, or of the fruits of faith.

<!-- p.91 -->

In these things we all have need of the prayer of the Blessed **Fulgentius**, which is after this manner:

> *"Of the one Master, and our Lord, Jesus Christ, I ask with my whole heart to be taught far more things which I know not, of whom I have received the few things which I know. Him I beseech that, His mercy going before and following after, whatsoever things wholesome to be known I know not, He would teach me. In those things which I know to be true, may He keep me; in those wherein, as a man, I am deceived, may He correct me. In those true things wherein I waver, may He confirm me: and from things false and hurtful may He deliver me; that in my thoughts and speeches He may find that which He wholesomely giveth, and may cause those things to proceed out of my mouth which are chiefly pleasing in His own sight, and so may be made acceptable to all the faithful. Amen."*

### *De confessione* · Of Confession

We have said that constancy pertaineth unto preaching. This is the true confession and profession of the true doctrine concerning God, which can be quenched or changed by no terrors, hatreds, or torments.

**Cyprian**: *"As often as I drink the blood of Christ, so often ought I to be ready for the shedding of mine own blood for Christ."* Consider therefore, whosoever thou art that hast been baptized, that thou art bound unto obedience and confession.

Now true confession is made in these ways. **I.** By the godliness and honesty of our life, of which Christ saith: *"Let your light shine before men."* **II.** By the furtherance of the ministry in Churches and Schools. For this is to nourish the Prophets of GOD. **III.** By frequent presence in the assembly where the true doctrine is set forth, **Psalm 84**: *"How amiable are thy tabernacles, O Lord of hosts."* **IIII.** By the profession of the true doctrine, as often as there is need, as it is written, **1 Pet. 3**: *"Be ready always to give an answer to every man that asketh you a reason of the hope that is in you, with meekness and reverence."* **V.** By constancy and Perseverance even unto the last Breath of life. **Matt. 10**: *"Whosoever shall confess me before men, <!-- p.92 --> him will I confess also before my Father which is in heaven."*

Here are to be considered the examples of the Apostles, confessors, martyrs, and others.

### *De iureiurando* · Of the Oath

An oath is nothing else than the invocation of Him who is the highest, that is, of God, who ought to be the witness of our speech, and ought to punish them that deceive. *"To swear is to call God to witness"*, saith **Jerome**. For we ask that God may be witness that we speak true things, and that He may punish, if wittingly we deceive. And so the oath is akin to the invocation of God, and often in Scripture the word *oath* is taken for invocation, as: *"All in that day shall swear by my name"*, that is, shall call upon it.

**Here it is asked, whether it be lawful for Christians to swear, seeing that Christ saith, Matt. 5: *"I say unto you, Swear not at all"*?**

**I answer** first to the sentence in Matthew: The whole sermon of Christ speaketh of each man's private cleanness; it speaketh not of commonwealths, and of the businesses of callings. He disalloweth therefore the oaths which are given without a calling, and without the commandment of the magistrate. Meanwhile He forbiddeth not to use the oath which the magistrate tendereth, or which a man's calling constraineth him to use. If the oath be ***ἐπακτὸν***, that is, laid upon one by the magistrate, to whom obedience is owed, it is just. Likewise: If the oath be made under the constraint of a calling, it is lawful, that we may defend either our own or other men's good name and life. All other oaths, which are not ***ἐπακτὰ***, and which are taken up without the necessity of a calling, let us know to be forbidden by Christ. And examples everywhere show that the oath is granted unto Christians. Paul sweareth divers times. **2 Cor. 1**: *"I call God for a record upon my soul."* **Rom. 1**: *"God is my witness, that I am always mindful of you."* **Heb. 6**: *"An oath is the end of all strife."* And the Law saith: *"Thou shalt swear by the name of thy God."* **Jer. 12**: *"They shall swear, The Lord liveth, and not by Baal."* God Himself also sweareth by <!-- p.93 --> Himself, **Ezek. 33**: *"As I live, I will not the death of a sinner."* So also Christ: *"Verily, verily, I say unto you."* And examples everywhere meet us in the Scriptures. And in the books of **Kings** the form of the oath is to be observed: *"The Lord do so to me, and more also"*, that is, May God take of me the most grievous punishments, if I shall deceive. It is an invocation, and an imprecation, whereby both God is cited as the witness of the things said, and a man is bound over unto punishment, if we deceive.

### *Quomodo se quis parare et gerere debet in iureiurando* · How a Man Ought to Prepare and Bear Himself in the Oath

**I.** The name of God alone is to be cited and called upon. **Deut. 6**: *"Thou shalt swear by the name of God"*, not by strange Gods, or by creatures. **Joshua 23**: *"Swear not by the name of the gods of the heathen."* **Jer. 12**: *"They shall swear, The Lord liveth, and not by Baal."* And in **Matt. 5** Christ most severely forbiddeth strange forms by creatures, by heaven and earth, to which invocation is not owed. Therefore the adversaries do ungodly, swearing by God and by all the Saints, whereas yet to swear is nothing else than to call upon God.

**II.** One must not forswear oneself against conscience. **Lev. 19**: *"Thou shalt not swear by my name falsely."* And there are many sentences of the Heathen, as **Phocylides**:

> ***Μήτ᾽ ἐπιορκήσεις μητ᾽ ἀγνῶς, μήτε ἑκώντι.***  
> Neither purely, or knowingly, nor of thine own will shalt thou forswear thyself.  

And out of **Homer** **Tibullus** taketh these verses, most worthy to be remembered:

> Ah wretch, even though a man at first conceal his perjuries,  
> Yet Punishment cometh late, with silent feet.  

And there is no doubt that dreadful punishments hang over men for perjuries, whereby both the glory of God is hurt, when God is cited as though a false witness, and God is despised by the perjurer, as though He would not avenge that dreadful ungodliness. Families <!-- p.94 --> are oftentimes blotted out for perjuries, as even the Heathen have observed, as **Juvenal**, *Satire* 13:

> To a certain Spartan the Pythian prophetess made answer, etc.  

It is reported that on a time a Jew disputed with a Christian, and that, the disputation having been long held, at length the Jew said: How cometh it to pass that more lame, blind and beggars are found among Christians than anywhere among the nations? When the Christian answered that he knew not, the other said: The Christians offend their God with too many perjuries, more than any other nations. Therefore also they are more punished by their God, because of perjuries and blasphemies. And since at this day perjuries are very common, grievous punishments, as well public as private, hang over us.

Now I will relate a notable example of perjury punished, out of the English history. When the English, after the death of King Canute the third, had cast off from their necks the servile yoke of the Danes,[^P04-5] and, the Danes themselves being partly slain and partly driven out of the Island, the Council had straightway thereafter sent for Alfred, the son of King Ethelred, to take the Kingdom — who at that time was in exile with his brother Edward at the court of William, Duke of Normandy — and Alfred, having received the message of the will of the Council, had hastily returned home, and it had pleased the English fathers that Earl Godwin himself should go to meet Alfred, now landed in England — a man factious, treacherous, deceitful and ambitious, but of great authority — it came to pass that, since he held it for certain that Alfred, a man of ready wit, would be a hindrance to him, that he should not excel in authority among the rest of the nobles, he determined that he must be taken out of the way. Having therefore gathered a very great band of armed men, he went forth to meet him, and slew the young man, who suspected no such thing, upon the way, with almost his whole company; and being returned to London he cleared himself to the Council of the deed in wondrous ways, and withal began to exhort that at the first day possible Edward, the brother of Alfred, should be sent for, and made King: for this he liked the better, because Edward was a good man, and a lover of simple truth, and in no wise shrewd, whom he reckoned <!-- p.95 --> would be such a Prince as he might with no trouble stand in favour with. Not very long after, Edward is called back into his country, who came to the wish of all, and was made King. After these things it so fell out that the King was on a time at dinner, and Earl Godwin sat at meat next unto him, when Harold, the Earl's Son, a young man far the most flourishing both in age and in virtue, who mixed the King's cup, gave Edward to drink, and, one foot by chance stumbling, came as near as might be to falling, and stayed himself by the help of the other foot, the wine being in no wise spilt out of the Goblet. Then his father Godwin, not without a jest: *Now*, saith he, *brother hath helped brother.* At that word the King, suddenly touched with longing for his brother Alfred, turning to the Earl, saith: *So would my brother have helped me his brother at all times, if thou alone hadst suffered it.* But in Godwin, from the conscience of his sin, there ariseth a sudden fear; who therefore excuseth himself by forswearing, saying: *If I have been the cause of thy brother's slaying, most holy King, I pray that by the will of God this bread may choke me.* Which words being spoken, he confidently taketh the bread, and forthwith, his throat being stopped, and death come upon him, he falleth to the ground. These things we have recited out of **Polydore**. And like examples are everywhere read in histories. The history is known of Rudolph, Duke of Swabia, who, having lost his hand in the battle at Merseburg, cried out: *This was the right hand wherewith I made oath to the Emperor Henry, which I was now the first to break.* This happened in the year of Christ 1080. How horrible is the history of the Overthrow at Varna, before which the Turk himself is reported to have cried out toward the cross: *Christ, if thou art the true Son of God, show now that the perjury of thy people pleaseth thee not.* The Turk therefore came off conqueror, and Vladislaus, King of Hungary, was killed, and the army of the Christians cut down with a miserable slaughter, in the year of Christ 1444. More examples, done even in our own age, I now pass over. But let us know that in every oath there is an imprecation. For God, who is cited as witness and approver,[^P04-6] is cited also as the judge of the oath, and the punisher and avenger of perjury. *If I swear falsely, may God punish me.* This imprecation is in every oath <!-- p.96 --> which either the glory of God, or the highest necessity, or the love of our neighbour requireth. Whence also it is read that the Romans sware, using this rite: He that was to swear held a flint stone in his hand, saying: *If wittingly I deceive, then may Diespiter, the city and citadel being safe, cast me out from my goods, as I cast this stone.*

Now in the oath let us think first of the true faith, whereunto we were bound in Baptism, and of the true doctrine, in which we ought to be constant even unto death: Next, of godliness of life, lest by indulging in wickedness we do against the oath which we have made unto God: Afterward, of the duties of mutual love toward our neighbour: Fourthly, of our calling. For we have each a certain calling, to which we are bound by oath, that we may be faithful Stewards. These things can be copiously set forth in their own place.

**III.** One must not swear lightly, nor in slight causes. **Sirach 23**: *"Accustom not thy mouth to swearing; he that sweareth lightly is not without sin, even as a servant that is often beaten is not without scars."* And here the magistrate ought to deal warily, that he lay not an oath upon any man lightly for any matter whatsoever, but let him know that an oath is a thing to be reverenced.

**Augustine** saith thus: *"He that provoketh a man to swear, and knoweth that he will swear falsely, surpasseth a murderer: because the murderer killeth the body, but he the soul, nay, two souls, both the soul of him whom he provoked to swear, and his own."*

**IIII.** The oath is to be kept, not broken, if it be not against God, and if it be possible; and indeed it is to be kept even with enemies: nay, with robbers and evil men. As Joshua keepeth faith with Rahab, because of the oath which the spies themselves had made, **Joshua 6**. Likewise: He keepeth faith with the Gibeonites, by whom nevertheless he had been deceived, **Joshua 9**. Therefore the Germans name it *Eyd* (oath), from the Hebrew *Ed*, that is, a firm covenant. And the Latins *Iuramentum*, from *Iuro*, which is from *ius* (right), that is, if the oath be just, and be not against God, and if the thing which is promised can be done.

<!-- p.97 -->

But when the oath is against God and His word, it is altogether to be broken. And rightly say the **Canonists**: *"In promises which cannot be performed without sin, the covenants are to be rescinded."* And the **Jurists** say: *"Let not an oath be a bond of iniquity."* As Saul would have done unjustly, if he had slain his son Jonathan because of the oath. For Jonathan knew nothing of his father's oath, and therefore did eat of the honey, **1 Sam. 14**. So Jephthah was not bound by his oath to sacrifice his daughter, **Judg. 11**. And the Wise Men return not unto Herod, although they had promised him that they would return. And Herod ought not to have kept the oath concerning the slaying of John. Therefore **Hugo** saith: *"He that sweareth that he will do an evil thing sinneth, and it is better then to break the oath than to fulfil it."* And this same thing we see that even the Heathen observed. Hippolytus, the son of Theseus, being asked by his stepmother, who was sick, to swear that he would do those things which she should lay upon him, and when he had sworn, and his stepmother sought to have her will of him, answered: *"With my tongue I sware, but my mind is unsworn."*

Here it is easily plain what is to be held concerning monastic vows. For all ungodly vows, and such as are made against the commandment of God, are void, do hurt the glory of Christ, and transfer the benefit of Christ unto works devised by men.

### *De peccatis contra II. Praeceptum* · Of the Sins against the Second Commandment

With invocation is at strife: **I.** The omission of invocation, and the security of drunken men, covetous men, etc., who pray neither for themselves nor for others. **II.** The false invocation of them who persevere in sins. Rightly saith **Isidore**: *"As no medicine availeth in a wound, if the iron be yet in it: so nothing availeth the prayer of him in whose mind guile, or in whose breast hatred, yet abideth."* **Prov. 15**: *"The Lord is far from the wicked, and heareth not the prayers of the wicked."* **III.** The invocation which is Heathen, Turkish, Jewish, the Idolatrous invocation of holy <!-- p.98 --> men that are dead, or the Papistical. For God alone is ***καρδιογνώστης*** (the knower of hearts), **Paralip. 6 [2 Chron. 6]**. And there is one only Mediator between God and mankind. And there is nothing commanded in the word of God concerning the invocation of the dead: whence also faith, determining that the invocation pleaseth God, cannot go before to give light. Therefore it is idolatry to call upon the dead. The Turks, although they say that they call upon God the Creator of heaven and earth, and of men, yet call not upon the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, and so they call not upon the true God, the Creator of heaven and earth. The true God is Father, Son and Holy Ghost. This one and triune God the Turks neither acknowledge nor call upon, albeit, against the judgement of their own reason, in the beginning of every work they recite the mystery of the Trinity, which they have set before every chapter of the Alcoran in this manner: *Bisem Allahe, el Rahmane, el Ruoahim.* That is, *In the name of God, of the God of mercy, of God their Spirit.*

Moreover, with Thanksgiving is at strife: **I.** Unthankfulness toward the benefits of God, spiritual and bodily, even as we are all unthankful, one more, another less; as the most wise King of Naples, **Alphonsus**, on a time said with a groan, when he had experience of the uttermost unthankfulness of his own, whom he had raised up and adorned with many benefits: *Why do I complain of the unthankfulness of others toward me, who toward so many and so great benefits of God am too unthankful?* Acknowledge the benefits of God, and give Him thanks, and shun sins. For he is unthankful who feareth not God. *"And in this every man becometh the more blameworthy, the more acceptable he is unto God, even as Adam sinned the more, because he abounded in every good"*, saith **Augustine**. And rightly **Chrysostom**: *"By how much the greater benefits are appointed unto men, by so much the heavier are the judgements upon them that sin."* Woe one day unto us Germans, whom hitherto God hath of His boundless goodness endowed with so many benefits, both spiritual and bodily, with the light of His Gospel, with the studies of the tongues and arts, with quiet dwellings. *"Evil shall not depart from the house of the unthankful."* How few are there who truly give thanks for the benefits bestowed by God upon themselves, much less for the benefits common to all?

<!-- p.99 -->

**II.** Feigned and hypocritical thankfulness, such as is that of the Pharisee, **Luke 18**, without the true fear of God, without the knowledge of his own self. We ought rather to consider what is wanting to us, than what is present; not that the gifts in us are not to be acknowledged, but that we ought not to fall into arrogance and pride. For there is nothing of ourselves that is good, which we have not received. To be wanting is of ourselves. But to be present is of God, if it be good.

With true Preaching are at strife all corruptions of doctrine, heresies. *Ketzerey* (heresy), from *Kezar*, that is, to separate. Likewise: Negligence in teaching, and curiosity in handling ***παρέργοις*** (things beside the purpose), unprofitable and unnecessary matters. And by every man who hath a care of godliness this Rule is to be observed: Whatsoever looketh not to the glory of God, and to thine own salvation or that of other men, is to be omitted in the doctrine of religion. For it is not necessary that many questions be moved concerning it, etc. Likewise: Whatsoever in the Church is at strife with any Commandment of the Decalogue, or Article of the Creed, howsoever neat and plausible it be, is to be detested and fled from.

With confession are at strife: **I.** Apostasy, or falling away, or denial, as of the Emperor Julian falling away to the Heathen, of Ecebolius the Rhetorician, of Francis Spira, and of others. **II.** The ungodly stubbornness of fanatical men under punishments, as of Servetus, and of the Anabaptists. Now Michael Servetus was burnt at Geneva in the year 1552,[^P04-7] who, when he came to the place of execution, William Farel exhorting him, would not call upon Christ the eternal Son of God, and though he gave no sign of repentance, yet did not at all defend his cause unto the people. Rightly saith **Cyprian**: *"The cause maketh the Martyr, not the martyrdom."* And **Apollinaris**: *"Where the truth of Christ is not, there is not the truth of martyrdom."*

With the oath are at strife, first, Sophistical and cavilling interpretations of oaths, as Hatto, Archbishop of Mainz, sweareth to Adelbert, Margrave of Bamberg, who for seven whole years had rebelled against King Louis, <!-- p.100 --> and could not be overcome by any force — he sweareth, I say, that he will bring him to the King for the making of peace, and will bring him back safe to his own home at Bamberg. The Margrave, being credulous, assenteth to the crafty Hatto. The next day Hatto, before they set forth, is bidden to dinner by the Prince, which he craftily refuseth, lest Adelbert also should take dinner, but that so, having mounted their horses, they may straightway enter upon the journey. Scarce were they gone forth, when Hatto saith that he is hungry, and that he hath not done wisely in neglecting the dinner that was offered, since he could not finish the whole journey fasting. He is therefore called back to dinner by the Margrave; they return into the castle out of which they had gone, and dine. Afterward they pursue the journey begun. When they come to the King, Adelbert is straightway condemned to lose his head; who, as he went to the place of execution, is reported to have put Hatto in mind of his promises and of his oath. This man answered that he was loosed from the oath, and had satisfied it; for that he had brought him back safe to his own home from the journey, to dinner, and that he had promised nothing of two bringings back. So the noble hero Adelbert is deprived of his head. This happened in the year of Christ 910. And hither belongeth almost all cavilling and sophistry, ***καὶ εὐτραπελία*** (and jesting) that is dishonest.

**II.** Ungodly oaths, whereby things dishonest, and forbidden of God, are promised.

**III.** Perjuries, blasphemies, abuses of the divine name, such as are those of magistrates, merchants, courtiers, soldiers, who, even as they walk with their hose slashed and torn, so with their tongue, which is the stamp of the mind, do slash and tear Christ, and His saving wounds.

We are all perjured before God. We all break the oath which we gave unto God in Baptism. God alone is true, to whose oath, *"As I live, I will not the death of a sinner"*, we must flee for refuge. Our perjuries we heal by the oath of God. Let these things be spoken to godly minds, not to the secure, and to them that are perjured against conscience without conversion, or true repentance.

[^P04-1]: The print reads *signa aeterna* ("eternal signs"), an evident misprint for *externa*.

[^P04-2]: The print reads *saltabant iterum* ("they danced again"); *interim* is translated.

[^P04-3]: The print reads *in Flagellaueris*.

[^P04-4]: So the print (*1. Reg. 40*); no book of Kings hath a fortieth chapter. Elsewhere on these pages Selnecker numbereth the four books of Kings after the Latin Bible, and the King James book is added in square brackets where it differeth.

[^P04-5]: The print hath *Dacorum* and *Dacis*, the common medieval Latin name for the Danes.

[^P04-6]: The print reads *approbatur* ("is approved"); *approbator* ("approver"), which the sense requires beside *testis* and *iudex*, is translated.

<!-- p.101 -->

**IIII.** There fighteth against the whole second Commandment the hypocrisy of those who say, *"Lord, Lord,"* and yet do not the will of the heavenly Father; that is, who neither have true faith, nor show forth their faith by love or good works. These take the name of God in vain, **Rom. 2**: *"Thou gloriest in the Law, and by transgression of the Law thou blasphemest God."* **2 Cor. 6**.

These sin horribly in these three things: **I.** They make the Word of God a cover for their own sins. **II.** They bring it to pass that by the adversaries, and by others, the true doctrine is blasphemed, and blamed as false, seeing there is not present a life agreeing with the doctrine. **III.** They are an evil example to others with whom they live.

All these there awaiteth the horrible threatening added to the second Commandment: God *will not hold them guiltless*, that is, He will certainly punish them, with bodily and eternal punishments. For He not only doth not hold them guiltless in this life, but also *will not* hold them guiltless: that we may know that the abuse of the divine name is punished with eternal punishments, unless remission be made, for the Mediator's sake, to those who are converted unto God.

The sum of the second Commandment is: **I.** To speak rightly and godly of God, and of all divine things, in teaching, in disputing, and in the whole life. **II.** To apply the Name of God to its true uses in life, etc.

# *Tertium Praeceptum* · The Third Commandment

The order of the Commandments is to be considered. The first Commandment teacheth the inward worships. The second preacheth of the outward profession, namely, invocation. Now the third, of ceremonies and the use of ceremonies.

But before we speak of the worships of the third Commandment, the word *Sabbath* is to be considered, which signifieth a ceasing, or rest; and in the Holy Scriptures there is set forth <!-- p.102 --> a fourfold Sabbath: the Sabbath of creation; the ceremonial or pedagogical Sabbath; the Sabbath of Redemption; the Sabbath of eternal life.

## *De Sabbatho Creationis* · Of the Sabbath of Creation

**Gen. 2**: *"God rested on the seventh day from all his work,"* that is, He created no new kind (*species*) in nature whose matter or likeness had not gone before. And He rested for this cause, that He might show that man, whom He had created last, after all the other creatures, is the uttermost and highest work, wherein He delighteth, and wherein He willeth to rest, and unto which the deliberation and desire of the whole workmanship was directed. For all things were created for the sake of men. And the end is more excellent than the things ordained unto it. For there were four chief causes for which God sanctified the Sabbath: The **first**, because He rested from the Creation of new kinds. The **second**, on account of the excellency of man. The **third**, for the instituting of a certain worship, in which the human race, even in its innocency — that is, even if Adam had not fallen — would have proclaimed the goodness of God, and would have rendered unto God acceptable worships, whereas on the other days other things would have had to be cared for. The **fourth**, for a testimony of immortality, and of a life eternal and better, into which man, even before the fall, would have been translated after a certain time had passed.

Yet God did not so rest as though He had departed from His work, as a Builder departeth from a house made ready and built up, and as though He had ceased to act — as the Epicureans think that there is no Providence; but He rested, as hath now been said, from the Creation of new kinds, not from preservation and sustaining. God is perpetually present with His creature, and most freely sustaineth and preserveth it, maketh the earth fruitful year by year, giveth life to living things, and the power of procreation, and in the Church doth from time to time work wonderful deliverances; **John 5**: *"My Father worketh hitherto, and I work."* **Acts 17**: *"In him we live, and move, and have our being,"* in God the Father, the Son, and the Holy <!-- p.103 --> Spirit. **Col. 1**: *"By him all things consist."* And there is this verse of **Vida**:

> God, filling all things, hath poured Himself into the whole mass.  

**Heb. 1**: *"Upholding all things by the word of his power."* And here the degrees of the divine presence are to be considered, of which the **first** is the general action of God, or universal presence, which is the presence of preservation, whereby God is present with all creatures in such wise that He preserveth them for so long as He willeth to preserve them; **Jer. 23**: *"Do not I fill heaven and earth? saith the Lord."* And of this degree there are many verses, as:

> By essence, by presence, God is, and everywhere by power.  

And our own:

> ***ἔστι θεὸς πάντῃ, ἐν καλοῖς τε κακοῖς τε,***  
> ***πάντα ἔχει, μοῦνος πάντα βλέπει τε θεός.***  

(God is everywhere, both among the good and among the evil; God holdeth all things, and alone seeth all things.)

**Lucan**:

> Jupiter is whatsoever thou seest, whithersoever thou movest.  

Likewise:

> And even a light sod is that which proveth that God is.  

And:

> And even a single herb showeth God to be present.  

And thus God is present even with the wicked. And it is certain that God is thus present with things *separably*, because He destroyeth the natures which He willeth to destroy, as Pharaoh, and the greatest part of the human race in the flood.

The **second** degree is that whereby God is present with regenerate and Holy men in this life, as with David, in such wise that He not only preserveth their life, but also worketh in them motions pleasing to Himself. And yet He is not beheld visibly, as He shall be beheld in the life eternal, and He is present separably.

The **third** degree is that whereby He is present with the blessed Angels and men in heaven, and preserveth not only their life, but also quickeneth them with life eternal, and filleth them with His own light and righteousness, and is beheld by them visibly and immediately; and although He departeth not from them, yet is He present with them by fellowship only. So shall God be all in all in the life eternal.

<!-- p.104 -->

The **fourth** degree is far other, whereby the divine nature in the Son of God, ***τῷ λόγῳ*** (the Word), is present with the human nature in such wise that there is a personal union, not only inseparably, but also after such a manner that there is one ***ὑφιστάμενον*** (subsisting thing) for ever, without end, as **Damascene** saith: *"The human nature, which the **λόγος** (Word) once assumed, He never forsaketh."* And so David prayeth, **1 Paral. 17 [1 Chron.]**: *"Thou hast looked upon me, O God, in the form of a man, who in the highest is the Lord God."*

## *De Sabbatho Ceremoniali* · Of the Ceremonial Sabbath

The Ceremonial, or pedagogical, or Mosaic Sabbath is that which is taught in the third Commandment, instituted for the sake of teaching, and that it may admonish us of the Sabbath to come. God willeth that on the seventh day there be a ceasing from the outward works of the body which hinder the ministry and the holy works; such as are, that others be taught concerning God, that the Sacraments be administered, etc.

Now seeing that the Ceremonial Laws delivered by Moses are abolished, it is here asked, whether the commandment of the Sabbath may be left out, and whether it be abrogated? I answer: The third commandment is mixed; it is partly Moral, as pertaineth to the *genus*; partly it is Ceremonial, as pertaineth to the *species*. The *genus* is the name given to the principal meaning of the commandment, or the final cause of the species instituted, that is, the work itself whereby the ceremonies which God commandeth are administered, and the ministry is preserved, and whereby every man contributeth his own office to the preservation of the public ministry. But the *species* is the name given to the certain day, and that the seventh. Thus therefore we say: I. That the Jews had a divine Commandment concerning the genus and concerning the species, or concerning the work and concerning the day. But seeing that we have a divine Commandment only concerning the work, and have not a Commandment concerning a certain day, therefore we say: That the genus which is contained in the third Commandment is in no wise abrogated, and, seeing it is moral, is perpetual and immutable; but that the species is abrogated, which is ceremonial. And for this cause the seventh <!-- p.105 --> day, which was the day of the Mosaic Sabbath, hath been transferred — the Apostles themselves being the authors, **Rev. 1** — by Christians unto the eighth day, which we name the Lord's day (*Dominicus*), because on that day the Son of God rose again from the dead: for which cause also the beginning of the day, which according to Moses the Jews begin from the evening time, we take from the morning time, calling to remembrance the glorious Resurrection of our Saviour, which came to pass in the morning.

Whence **Ignatius** saith excellently, **Epist. to the Magnesians 3**: ***μετὰ τὸ σαββατίσαι*** (that is, after that the Jewish Sabbath hath now been abrogated), ***ἑορταζέτω πᾶς φιλόχριστος τὴν κυριακὴν, τὴν ἀναστάσιμον, τὴν βασιλίδα, τὴν ὕπατον πασῶν τῶν ἡμερῶν. ἣν περιμένων ὁ προφήτης, ἔλεγεν· εἰς τὸ τέλος, ὑπὲρ τῆς ὀγδόης. ἐν ᾗ καὶ ἡ ζωὴ ἡμῶν ἀνέτειλε, καὶ τοῦ θανάτου γέγονε νίκη ἐν τῷ χριστῷ.*** (After the keeping of the Sabbath, let every lover of Christ keep as a feast the Lord's day, the day of the resurrection, the queen, the chief of all the days; in expectation whereof the prophet said: "Unto the end, for the eighth"; on which also our life arose, and the victory over death was wrought in Christ.)

Now it is to be noted by the younger sort, that of the commandments of God some are Catholic, namely, those which bind all men and every man, either to obey or to suffer punishment; but that others are particular, that is, given to one nation only or to one man only; concerning which three Rules are to be observed. The **first**: No Commandment is in all respects so particular, but that it also in some measure bindeth all men, to wit, as pertaineth to the genus. The **second**: Whatsoever of outward circumstances — that is, of time, person, place, number, order, and other like things — is annexed to particular commandments, all that is to be left to those unto whom those things were commanded particularly and by name, or in the species. The **third**: Whatsoever, the outward circumstances being now set aside, remaineth of the genus, that is, of true faith and charity (for these two are the theses and the mark of the whole Christian doctrine, profession and life), that is plainly to be transferred unto all men and every man, and to be fitted to them with a firm application. Now from these things it followeth, that the ceremonial observance of the Sabbath is left to the Jews, but that the Moral, which requireth faith and charity, pertaineth unto us. And of the ceremony Paul speaketh, saying, **Col. 2**: *"Let no man judge you in respect of an holyday,"* to wit, a <!-- p.106 --> Ceremonial one. For Paul speaketh not of the genus, or the principal meaning of the commandment, and the final cause, which is natural and immutable.

## *De Sabbatho Redemptionis* · Of the Sabbath of Redemption

This is the Sabbath of the Son of God resting after an unspeakable manner in the sepulchre, the whole seventh day, as though He had been forsaken of God. In this manner God hath restored unto us the Feast of the Sabbath, which was lost in the fall. And as the Son of God, before the honour of the Sabbath to come, first rested in death, so the whole Church beginneth the honour of the Sabbath from a like rest or ceasing, that is, from death; wherein although we seem to be forsaken, yet shall we certainly be raised again unto the honour of the eternal Sabbath.

## *De Sabbatho Vitae Aeternae* · Of the Sabbath of Eternal Life

**Heb. 3 and 4**: *"There remaineth a Sabbath to the people."* **Isa. 66**: *"There shall be Sabbath after Sabbath"*; in which, namely, all the offices of the animal life ceasing, without darkness and sin, without any labour and pain, we shall do only the works of the Sabbath, we shall behold God face to face and celebrate Him, and He Himself shall impart unto us the fellowship of His own light and of eternal life. This future and eternal rest the type in the Law of the Sabbath shadoweth forth, as we shall say more fully below.

## *De Numero Septenario* · Of the Number Seven

That the number seven is Sacred, and is so named, cometh to pass for this cause: that God also rested on the seventh day, and that the Holy Scriptures very often use that number, and that experience testifieth that this number is altogether peculiar in the whole of life, in the changes and vicissitudes of kingdoms, of diseases, of the state of life, etc. Hence the critical days, hence the climacteric years, <!-- p.107 --> hence certain fixed periods of some Commonwealths and kingdoms, and other like things well-nigh infinite, many of which **Macrobius** reciteth in the *Dream of Scipio*. For the Heathen also observed this, as **Pythagoras** called the number seven the vehicle and complement of human life. For it comprehendeth body and soul. The body consisteth of four elements. The soul hath a threefold power, ***λογικὴν, θυμικὴν, καὶ ἐπιθυμητικήν*** (the rational, the irascible, and the concupiscible). In Civil matters seven witnesses make credit sure, even as at this day also in the Empire there are seven Electors. And the verses of the Poet **Linus** are these:

> When the seventh Light came, the Almighty Father began  
> To finish all things: and the seventh is among the good, and the selfsame  
> Seventh is also the beginning of all things.  
> The seventh is the same as the first, and the seventh is perfect in sevens:  
> Whence also the high heaven with its wandering stars  
> Is turned, and is borne round about on every side in as many circles.  

Thus therefore they have numbered seven Planets; seven liberal arts; seven distinctions of notes in Music; ***καὶ ἑπτάχορδον*** (and the seven-stringed) Lyre of Mercury; seven ***φάσεις*** (phases) of the Moon in one whole month. So also the Moon herself accomplisheth the Zodiac in 28 days, which consist of four times seven days. For in seven days she cometh from the Northern half to the midpoint of latitude, that is, to the ecliptic place. From which in the seven following she glideth down to the lowest Southern point; again in another seven she climbeth slantwise to the middle; in the last seven she is restored to the Northern summit. But who could number all? **Macrobius**, **Gellius**, and others are to be consulted, who discourse learnedly of these things.

The Holy Scriptures use this number as a certain number for an uncertain, indifferently, and that in the place of multitude and fulness. For **שבע**, *Sebagh*, signifieth fulness and sufficiency: as, *"The just man falleth seven times in a day,"* **Prov. 24**. *"Until the barren bare seven,"* **1 Sam. 2**, that is, many. *"Seven times a day do I praise thee,"* **Ps. 119**. *"The sluggard is wiser in his own conceit than seven men that can render a reason,"* that is, <!-- p.108 --> than many others, **Prov. 26**. *"She that hath borne seven languisheth,"* **Jer. 15**. Elias sendeth his servant seven times to look for the rain, **3 Kings 17 [1 Kings]**. Elisha biddeth Naaman the Syrian wash seven times in Jordan, **4 Kings 5 [2 Kings]**. Hezekiah sacrificeth seven bullocks, seven rams, seven lambs, seven he goats, **2 Paral. 27 [2 Chron.]**. The Church in the time of Hezekiah, after the cleansing of the temple, slew seventy oxen. Asa, seven hundred oxen, and of sheep seven thousand, **2 Paral. 15 [2 Chron.]**. A fault is to be forgiven a neighbour, saith Christ, not seven times, that is, not often, but seventy times seven, that is, always and everywhere. And in Daniel, after the Babylonish captivity, which lasted 70 years, there are numbered seventy weeks unto Christ. The seventh day from the creation was the Sabbath, or rest of God, **Gen. 1**. The seventh day is our ceremonial Sabbath. On the seventh day, or Sabbath, Christ rested in the sepulchre. The seventh thousand of the world shall bring forth the eternal Sabbath, as the saying of Elias testifieth; whereunto pertaineth the oracle of **Orpheus**, which **Plato** allegeth:

> ***Ἕκτῃ ἐν γενεῇ κόσμος καταπαύσετ' ἀοιδῆς.***  

(In the sixth generation the world shall cease from its song.)

And **Irenaeus** recordeth the same concerning the six days of Creation, applied to the six thousands of the world. But thus much of these things. Let the studious, however, themselves gather examples out of **Clement** and other authors, of which there is assuredly a great number in nature. Seven spans make up the length of the human body. The life of man is of seven days, as touching his beginning, and his extinction which cometh by fasting, saith **Hippocrates**. The diameter of a circle is compared to the circumference by the number seven. The days of the week, as touching the names of the Planets which have been given to them, consist of an odd number, to wit, of seven; the seven Planets being numbered thrice every day and fitted to the hours, as though they were thrice seven, and three being added besides, which, when seven days are finished, make three sevens, and four being reserved for the numbering of the hours of the day following, which, when seven days are ended, make up four sevens. <!-- p.109 --> Now it pleaseth me to add the Elegy of **Solon**, which **Clement of Alexandria**, ***στρωμάτ.*** (*Stromata*) book 6, and **Philo** in the Oration *On the Workmanship of the World*, do recite:

> ***Παῖς μὲν ἄνηβος ἐὼν ἔτι νήπιος ἕρκος ὀδόντων***  
> ***Φύσας ἐκβάλλει πρῶτον ἐν ἕπτ' ἔτεσιν.***  
> ***Τοὺς δ' ἑτέρους ὅτε δὴ τελέσει θεώς ἕπτ' ἐνιαυτοὺς,***  
> ***Ἥβης ἐκφαίνει σπέρματα γινομένης.***  
> ***Τῇ τριτάτῃ δὲ γένειον ἀεξομένων ἐπὶ γυίων***  
> ***Λαχνοῦται χροίης ἄνθος ἀμειβομένης.***  
> ***Τῇ δὲ τετάρτῃ πᾶς τὶς ἐν ἑβδομάδι μέγ' ἄριστος***  
> ***Ἰσχὺν, ἥν τ' ἄνδρες σήματ' ἔχουσ' ἀρετῆς.***  
> ***Πέμπτῃ δ' ὥριον ἄνδρα γάμου μεμνημένον εἶναι,***  
> ***Καὶ παίδων ζητεῖν εἰς ὀπίσω γενεήν.***  
> ***Τῇ δ' ἕκτῃ περὶ πάντα καταρτύεται νόος ἀνδρὸς***  
> ***Οὐδ' ἐτιδεῖν ἔθ' ὅμως ἔργα μάταια θέλει.***[^P05-4]  
> ***Ἑπτὰ δὲ νοῦν καὶ γλῶσσαν ἐν ἑβδομάσι μέγ' ἄριστος,***  
> ***Ὀκτώ δ' ἀμφοτέρων τέσσαρα καὶ δέκ' ἔτη.***  
> ***Τῇ δ' ἐνάτῃ ἔτι μὲν δύναται μετριώτερα δ' αὐτοῦ***  
> ***Πρὸς μεγάλην ἀρετὴν σῶμά τε καὶ δύναμις.***  
> ***Τῇ δεκάτῃ δ' ὅτε δὴ τελέσει θεὸς ἕπτ' ἐνιαυτοὺς,***  
> ***Οὐκ ἂν ἄωρος ἐὼν μοῖραν ἔχει θανάτου.***  

That is, as a certain man hath rendered it:

> After the infant hath fulfilled seven years,  
> The teeth are brought forth, to be as a wall of the mouth:  
> After God hath granted him another seven years,  
> He cometh to ripeness, and the seed of generation is given him.
